Xushan Huang Xushan Huang Department of Mathematics Yonsei University, Seoul 03722, Republic of Korea Email address: xushanhuang@yonsei.ac.kr , Moon-Jin Kang Moon-Jin Kang Department of Mathematical Sciences Korea Advanced Institute of Science and Technology, Daejeon 34141, Republic of Korea Email address: moonjinkang@kaist.ac.kr , Hobin Lee Hobin Lee Department of Mathematical Sciences Korea Advanced Institute of Science and Technology, Daejeon 34141, Republic of Korea Email address: lcuh11@kaist.ac.kr and HyeonSeop Oh HyeonSeop Oh Department of Mathematical Sciences Korea Advanced Institute of Science and Technology, Daejeon 34141, Republic of Korea Email address: ohs2509@kaist.ac.kr Date: August 24, 2026 Abstract. We study the time-asymptotic stability of solutions to the inflow problem for the one-dimensional Navier–Stokes–Fourier system on the half-line. We consider the most generic wave pattern: the superposition of a degenerate boundary layer, a rarefaction, a viscous contact wave, and a viscous shock. More precisely, if the boundary data belongs to the subsonic region, and the initial perturbation and strengths of the boundary layer, viscous contact wave, and viscous shock are sufficiently small, then the solution to the inflow problem converges to the corresponding superposition, up to a time-dependent shift for a shock. The rarefaction wave, however, is allowed to have arbitrarily large strength. To control the viscous shock, we employ the method of $a$ -contraction with shifts. A notable feature of our analysis is that this method can be applied even when the rarefaction wave has large amplitude. In particular, this resolves, in a generic setting, the open problem of the stability of inflow wave patterns containing a viscous shock for Navier–Stokes–Fourier system. Key words and phrases: Navier–Stokes–Fourier system; Inflow problem; Riemann Problem; Viscous shock; Boundary layer; Stability 2020 Mathematics Subject Classification 35Q35, 76N06 Contents 1 Introduction 1.1 Main Result 1.2 Previous Results 1.3 Idea of Proof 1.4 Organization of the Paper 2 Preliminaries 2.1 Boundary Layer Solution 2.2 Approximate Rarefaction Wave 2.3 Viscous Contact Wave 2.4 Viscous Shock Wave 2.5 Poincaré-type Inequality 3 A Priori Estimates and Proof of Main Theorem 3.1 Local Existence 3.2 Construction of the Shift and the Weight Function 3.3 A Priori Estimates 3.4 Proof of Theorem 1.1 3.5 Notations 4 Wave Interaction Estimates 4.1 $L^{1}$ Interaction Estimates 4.2 $L^{2}$ Interaction Estimates 5 Zeroth Order Estimates 5.1 Relative Entropy Estimates 5.2 Decompositions 5.2.1 Decomposition of $\mathcal{J}^{H}$ 5.2.2 Decomposition of $Y$ 5.3 Leading Order Estimates 5.4 Estimates of the Remainder Terms 5.4.1 Estimates of $B^{rem}(U)$ and $B^{C}(U)$ 5.4.2 Estimates of $B^{I}(U)$ 5.4.3 Estimates of $\mathcal{J}^{bd}$ 5.4.4 Conclusion 6 Higher Order Estimates 6.1 Higher Order Estimates on $v-\bar{v}$ 6.2 Higher Order Estimates on $u-\bar{u}$ 6.3 Higher Order Estimates on $\theta-\bar{\theta}$ A Proof of Lemma B Proof of Lemma C Proof of Global Existence References 1. Introduction We consider the inflow problem of the one-dimensional Navier–Stokes–Fourier (NSF) system on the half-line $\mathbb{R}_{+}:=(0,\infty)$ : (1.1) $\begin{cases}&\rho_{t}+(\rho u)_{x}=0,\quad x\geq 0,\quad t\geq 0,\\
&(\rho u)_{t}+(\rho u^{2}+p)_{x}=\mu u_{xx},\\
&\mathcal{E}_{t}+\left(u\mathcal{E}+pu\right)_{x}=\kappa\theta_{xx}+\mu(uu_{x})_{x}\\
&(\rho,u,\theta)(t,0)=(\rho_{-},u_{-},\theta_{-})\quad\text{with}\quad\rho_{-},u_{-},\theta_{-}>0,\end{cases}$ subject to the initial data $(\rho_{0},u_{0},\theta_{0})(x)=(\rho,u,\theta)(0,x),\quad\text{with}\quad\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}\rho_{0}(x)>0,\quad\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}\theta_{0}(x)>0,$ and the far-field condition $\left(\rho,u,\theta\right)(0,x)\to(\rho_{+},u_{+},\theta_{+})\quad\mbox{as}\quad{x}\to\infty.$ Here, $\rho=\rho(t,x),u=u(t,x)$ and $\theta=\theta(t,x)$ represent the fluid density, velocity and absolute temperature respectively, $\mathcal{E}=\rho(e+\frac{u^{2}}{2})$ is the total energy function. For the ideal polytropic gas, the pressure function $p$ and the internal energy function $e$ are given by $p(\rho,\theta)=R\rho\theta=A\rho^{\gamma}\exp\left(\frac{\gamma-1}{R}s\right),\qquad e(\rho,\theta)=\frac{R}{\gamma-1}\theta+const,$ where $s$ is the (physical) entropy, $A>0,R>0$ , $\gamma>1$ being both constant related to the fluid, while $\mu$ and $\kappa$ denote the viscosity and the heat-conductivity. In Lagrangian mass coordinates, the inflow problem ( 1.1 ) can be rewritten as (1.2) $\begin{cases}&v_{t}-u_{x}=0,\quad t>0,\quad x>\sigma_{-}t,\\
&u_{t}+p(v,\theta)_{x}=\left(\mu\frac{u_{x}}{v}\right)_{x},\\
&E_{t}+(p(v,\theta)u)_{x}=\left(\kappa\frac{\theta_{x}}{v}\right)_{x}+\left(\mu\frac{uu_{x}}{v}\right)_{x},\\
&(v,u,\theta)(t,\sigma_{-}t)=(v_{-},u_{-},\theta_{-}),\quad v_{-},u_{-},\theta_{-}>0,\end{cases}$ where $v(t,x)=1/\rho$ represents the specific volume, $v_{-}=\frac{1}{\rho_{-}},\sigma_{-}=-\frac{u_{-}}{v_{-}}<0,p(v,\theta)=\frac{R\theta}{v}$ , and $E=e+\frac{u^{2}}{2}$ denotes the specific total energy. Moreover, the far-field condition is reformulated by $(v,u,\theta)(0,x)\to(v_{+},u_{+},\theta_{+})\quad\mbox{as}\quad{x}\to\infty.$ We are interested in the long-time dynamics of solutions $U=(v,u,\theta)$ to the inflow problem ( 1.2 ). It is well known that these asymptotic patterns for the NSF system are closely related to the Riemann problem for the full Euler system (the NSF system with $\mu=\kappa=0$ ). For the Cauchy problem on the whole line $\mathbb{R}$ , one of the generic asymptotic wave patterns consists of a rarefaction wave, a viscous contact wave, a viscous shock, or their superpositions. However, for the inflow problem, an additional asymptotic pattern, namely, a boundary layer solution (stationary solution), appears due to the presence of the boundary. Indeed, the asymptotic wave patterns for the inflow problem are determined by the locations of the boundary state $U_{-}$ and the far-field state $U_{+}$ . To describe these wave patterns, we define the sound speed $c(\theta)=\sqrt{R\gamma\theta}$ and decompose the state space into the subsonic, transonic, and supersonic regions: $\displaystyle\Omega_{sub}$ $\displaystyle:=\{(v,u,\theta)\,:\,|u|<c(\theta)\},$ $\displaystyle\Gamma_{trans}$ $\displaystyle:=\{(v,u,\theta)\,:\,|u|=c(\theta)\},$ $\displaystyle\Omega_{super}$ $\displaystyle:=\{(v,u,\theta)\,:\,|u|>c(\theta)\}.$ Since we consider the inflow problem, we restrict our attention to the regions with positive velocity and define $\Omega_{sub}^{+},\Gamma_{trans}^{+}$ , and $\Omega_{super}^{+}$ by $\Omega_{sub}^{+}:=\Omega_{sub}\cap\{u>0\},\quad\Gamma_{trans}^{+}:=\Gamma_{trans}\cap\{u>0\},\quad\Omega_{super}^{+}=\Omega_{super}\cap\{u>0\}.$ We consider the case where $U_{-}\in\Omega_{sub}^{+}$ . In particular, we consider the case where the asymptotic behavior of solutions to the inflow problem ( 1.2 ) is given as a superposition of a degenerate boundary layer solution, a rarefaction wave, a viscous contact wave, and a viscous shock wave, which are described below. Before describing these waves, we first introduce the change of variables $(t,\xi):=(t,x-\sigma_{-}t)$ to fix the boundary. Then, the system ( 1.2 ) can be written in terms of $(t,\xi)$ as (1.3) $\begin{cases}&v_{t}-\sigma_{-}v_{\xi}-u_{\xi}=0,\quad t>0,\quad\xi>0,\\
&u_{t}-\sigma_{-}u_{\xi}+p(v,\theta)_{\xi}=\left(\mu\frac{u_{\xi}}{v}\right)_{\xi},\\
&E_{t}-\sigma_{-}E_{\xi}+(p(v,\theta)u)_{\xi}=\left(\kappa\frac{\theta_{\xi}}{v}\right)_{\xi}+\left(\mu\frac{uu_{\xi}}{v}\right)_{\xi},\\
&(v,u,\theta)(t,0)=(v_{-},u_{-},\theta_{-}),\quad v_{-},u_{-},\theta_{-}>0,\end{cases}$ subject to the initial data and the far-field condition (1.4) $(v,u,\theta)(0,\xi)=(v_{0},u_{0},\theta_{0})(\xi),\quad\lim_{\xi\to\infty}(v,u,\theta)(0,\xi)=(v_{+},u_{+},\theta_{+}).$ $\bullet$ Boundary layer solution: For a given $U_{*}\in\Gamma_{trans}^{+}$ , [ 44 ] proved that there exists a curve $\Sigma\subset\{(u,\theta)\,:\,u>0,\,\,\theta>0\}$ , such that, for any $U_{-}\in BL(U_{*})$ , there exists a unique (degenerate) boundary layer solution (stationary solution) $U^{BL}=U^{BL}(\xi)$ to ( 1.3 ) satisfying (1.5) $\begin{cases}&-\sigma_{-}v^{BL}_{\xi}-u^{BL}_{\xi}=0,\quad\xi>0,\\
&-\sigma_{-}u^{BL}_{\xi}+p(v^{BL},\theta^{BL})_{\xi}=\left(\mu\frac{u^{BL}_{\xi}}{v^{BL}}\right)_{\xi},\\
&-\sigma_{-}\left(\frac{R}{\gamma-1}\theta^{BL}+\frac{(u^{BL})^{2}}{2}\right)_{\xi}+\big(p(v^{BL},\theta^{BL})u^{BL}\big)_{\xi}=\left(\kappa\frac{\theta^{BL}_{\xi}}{v^{BL}}\right)_{\xi}+\left(\mu\frac{u^{BL}u^{BL}_{\xi}}{v^{BL}}\right)_{\xi},\\
&(v^{BL},u^{BL},\theta^{BL})(0)=(v_{-},u_{-},\theta_{-}),\quad(v^{BL},u^{BL},\theta^{BL})(+\infty)=(v_{*},u_{*},\theta_{*}).\end{cases}$ Here, the boundary layer curve $BL(U_{*})$ is defined by $BL(U_{*}):=\left\{(v,u,\theta)\,:\,\frac{u}{v}=\frac{u_{*}}{v_{*}}=-\sigma_{-},\quad(u,\theta)\in\Sigma\right\}\subset\Omega_{sub}^{+}.$ $\bullet$ 1-rarefaction wave and approximate 1-rarefaction wave: We consider the situation in which the state $U_{*}$ and the far-field state $U_{+}$ are connected by a 1-rarefaction wave, contact discontinuity, and 3-shock wave, with intermediate states $U_{m}$ and $U^{*}$ . First, we consider $U_{m}\in R_{1}(U_{*})$ , where $R_{1}(U_{*})$ is a 1-rarefaction curve. Namely, $R_{1}(U_{*})$ the integral curve of the first characteristic field: $\displaystyle R_{1}(U_{*}):=\bigg\{(v,u,\theta)\,:\,v>v_{*},\,\,s(v,\theta)=s(v_{*},\theta_{*}):=s(v_{m},\theta_{m}),$ $\displaystyle\phantom{R_{1}(U_{*}):=\bigg\{(v,u,\theta)\,:\,\,}u=u_{*}-\int_{v_{*}}^{v}\lambda_{1}(s_{*},\eta)\,d\eta\bigg\},$ where (1.6) $s(v,\theta)=\frac{R}{\gamma-1}\ln\left(\frac{R}{A}\theta v^{\gamma-1}\right)$ denotes the entropy, and $\lambda_{1}(s,v)=-\sqrt{\frac{\gamma p(v,s)}{v}}$ is the first eigenvalue of the Jacobian of the hyperbolic part of ( 1.2 ). Then, for any $U_{m}\in R_{1}(U_{*})$ , the solution to the Riemann problem of the Euler system (1.7) $\begin{cases}&v_{t}-u_{x}=0,\quad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R},\\
&u_{t}+p(v,\theta)_{x}=0,\\
&E_{t}+(p(v,\theta)u)_{x}=0,\\
\end{cases}$ with the initial data $(v,u,\theta)(0,x)=\begin{cases}(v_{*},u_{*},\theta_{*}),\,\,&\text{if }x<0,\\
(v_{m},u_{m},\theta_{m}),\,\,&\text{if }x>0,\\
\end{cases}$ is given by a 1-rarefaction wave $U^{r}(t,x)$ , defined by $\lambda_{1}(v^{r}(t,x),\theta^{r}(t,x))=\begin{cases}\lambda_{1}(v_{*},\theta_{*}),\,\,&\text{if }x<\lambda_{1}(v_{*},\theta_{*})t,\\
\frac{x}{t},\,\,&\text{if }\lambda_{1}(v_{*},\theta_{*})t<x<\lambda_{1}(v_{m},\theta_{m})t,\\
\lambda_{1}(v_{m},\theta_{m}),\,\,&\text{if }x>\lambda_{1}(v_{m},\theta_{m})t,\end{cases}$ together with $\displaystyle Z_{1}(v^{r}(t,x),u^{r}(t,x);s^{r})=Z_{1}(v_{*},u_{*};s^{r})=Z_{1}(v_{m},u_{m};s^{r}),$ $\displaystyle s(v^{r}(t,x),\theta^{r}(t,x))=s(v_{*},\theta_{*})=s(v_{m},\theta_{m})=:s^{r},$ where $Z_{1}(v,u;s):=u+\int^{v}\lambda_{1}(s,v^{\prime})dv^{\prime}$ , and $s$ , defined in ( 1.6 ), are Riemann invariants associated with the first characteristic field. Due to the lack of regularity of the rarefaction wave $U^{r}$ at the rarefaction fronts, we introduce the following smooth approximation of $U^{r}$ as in [ 16 ] . First, we define the smooth solution $w(t,x)=w(t,x;w_{-},w_{+})$ of the Burgers equation $\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}w_{t}+ww_{x}=0,\\
w(0,x)=w_{0}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}w_{-},\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\,x<0,\\
w_{-}+C_{q}\delta_{r}\int^{\varepsilon_{r}x}_{0}y^{q}e^{-y}\,dy,\quad x\geq 0,\end{array}\right.\end{array}\right.$ where $\delta_{r}=w_{+}-w_{-}$ , $q$ is a constant satisfying $q\geq 8$ to be specified later, and $C_{q}$ is defined by $C_{q}\int^{\infty}_{0}y^{q}e^{-y}dy=1.$ Here, $\varepsilon_{r}<1$ is a sufficiently small constant to be chosen later. We now construct the smooth approximate 1-rarefaction wave $U^{AR}(t,x)$ corresponding to the 1-rarefaction wave $U^{r}(x/t)$ by (1.13) $\begin{cases}&\lambda_{1}(v^{AR},\theta^{AR})(t,x)=w(1+t,x;\lambda_{1}((v_{*},\theta_{*})),\lambda_{1}((v_{m},\theta_{m}))),\\
&Z_{1}(v^{AR}(t,x),u^{AR}(t,x);s^{r})=Z_{1}(v_{*},u_{*};s^{r})=Z_{1}(v_{m},u_{m};s^{r}),\\
&s(v^{AR}(t,x),\theta^{AR}(t,x))=s(v_{*},\theta_{*})=s(v_{m},\theta_{m})=s^{r}.\end{cases}$ Then, one can verify that the approximate 1-rarefaction wave $U^{AR}$ satisfies the Euler system ( 1.7 ). Moreover, $U^{AR}$ indeed approximates the self-similar rarefaction wave $U^{r}$ in the sense that (see Lemma 2.3 ): $\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left|(v^{AR},u^{AR},\theta^{AR})(t,x)-(v^{r},u^{r},\theta^{r})\left(\frac{x}{1+t}\right)\right|\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad t\to\infty.$ We then define a approximate 1-rarefaction wave $U^{R}$ on the half-line $\mathbb{R}_{+}$ as the restriction of $U^{AR}$ : (1.14) $(v^{R},u^{R},\theta^{R})(t,\xi):=(v^{AR},u^{AR},\theta^{AR})(t,\xi+\sigma_{-}t).$ $\bullet$ Viscous contact wave: Next, we consider $U^{*}\in CD(U_{m})$ , where $CD(U_{m})$ denotes the 2-contact discontinuity curve defined by (1.15) $CD(U_{m}):=\big\{(v,u,\theta)\,:\,u=u_{m},\,\,p(v,\theta)=p(v_{m},\theta_{m})\big\}.$ Since the second characteristic field of the Euler system ( 1.7 ) is linearly degenerate, the corresponding Riemann solution for the Euler system ( 1.7 ) with the initial data $(v,u,\theta)(0,x)=\begin{cases}(v_{m},u_{m},\theta_{m}),\quad&\text{if }x<0,\\
(v^{*},u^{*},\theta^{*}),\quad&\text{if }x>0,\end{cases}$ is given by a contact discontinuity $U^{c}$ defined by $(v^{c},u^{c},\theta^{c})(t,x)=\begin{cases}(v_{m},u_{m},\theta_{m}),\quad&\text{if }x<0,\\
(v^{*},u^{*},\theta^{*}),\quad&\text{if }x>0.\end{cases}$ In the viscous setting, for any $U^{*}\in CD(U_{m})$ , we define a viscous contact wave $U^{VC}(t,x)$ on $\mathbb{R}$ associated with the contact discontinuity $U^{c}$ by (1.16) $\begin{cases}v^{VC}\left(t,x\right)=\frac{R\Theta^{sim}}{p^{*}},\\
u^{VC}\left(t,x\right)=u^{*}+\frac{(\gamma-1)\kappa\Theta_{x}^{sim}}{R\gamma\Theta^{sim}},\\
\theta^{VC}\left(t,x\right)=\Theta^{sim},\end{cases}$ where $p^{*}:=p(v^{*},\theta^{*})$ and $\Theta^{sim}=\Theta^{sim}\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt{1+t}}\right)$ is the unique self-similar solution to the nonlinear diffusion equation: $\begin{cases}\Theta_{t}=\frac{(\gamma-1)\kappa p^{*}}{R^{2}\gamma}\left(\frac{\Theta_{x}}{\Theta}\right)_{x},\quad t>0,x\in\mathbb{R},\\
\Theta(t,-\infty)=\theta_{m},\quad\Theta(t,\infty)=\theta^{*}.\end{cases}$ We refer to [ 14 ] for the derivation of the viscous contact wave $U^{VC}$ . We also remark that, for any $p\geq 1$ (see ( 2.8 ) 1 and ( 2.8 ) 2 ), $\left\|(v^{C},u^{C},\theta^{C})(t,\cdot)-(v^{c},u^{c},\theta^{c})(t,\cdot)\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R})}=O(1)\kappa^{\frac{1}{2p}}(1+t)^{\frac{1}{2p}},\quad\forall t>0.$ Consequently, the viscous contact wave converges to the inviscid contact discontinuity in $L^{p}$ as $\kappa\to 0+$ on any finite time interval. However, the two profiles may not remain close for large time. Then, we define a viscous contact wave $U^{C}$ on the half-line $\mathbb{R}_{+}$ as the restriction of $U^{VC}$ : (1.17) $(v^{C},u^{C},\theta^{C})(t,\xi):=(v^{VC},u^{VC},\theta^{VC})(t,\xi+\sigma_{-}t).$ $\bullet$ Viscous 3-shock wave: Finally, we consider $U_{+}\in S_{3}(U^{*})$ , where $S_{3}(U^{*})$ denotes the Hugoniot locus associated with the third characteristic field. More precisely, $S_{3}(U^{*})$ consists of all states $U=(v,u,\theta)$ for which there exists a shock speed $\sigma$ satisfying Rankine Hugoniot condition and Lax entropy condition $\begin{aligned} &-\sigma(v-v^{*})-(u-u^{*})=0,\\
&-\sigma(u-u^{*})+(p(v,\theta)-p(v^{*},\theta^{*}))=0,\\
&-\sigma(E-E^{*})+(p(v,\theta)u-p(v^{*},\theta^{*})u^{*})=0,\end{aligned}\qquad v^{*}>v,\quad\sigma=\sqrt{-\frac{p(v,\theta)-p(v^{*},\theta^{*})}{v-v^{*}}}.$ For any $U_{+}\in S_{3}(U^{*})$ , a viscous 3-shock wave $U^{S}$ , which is the viscous counterpart of the inviscid shock wave, is a traveling wave $U^{S}(\zeta)=U^{S}(x-\sigma t)$ of ( 1.2 ) satisfying (1.18) $\begin{cases}&-\sigma(v^{S})^{\prime}-(u^{S})^{\prime}=0,\quad^{\prime}=\frac{d}{d\zeta},\\
&-\sigma(u^{S})^{\prime}+p(v^{S},\theta^{S})^{\prime}=\left(\mu\frac{(u^{S})^{\prime}}{v^{S}}\right)^{\prime},\\
&-\sigma(E^{S})^{\prime}+(p(v^{S},\theta^{S})u^{S})^{\prime}=\left(\kappa\frac{(\theta^{S})^{\prime}}{v^{S}}\right)^{\prime}+\left(\mu\frac{u^{S}(u^{S})^{\prime}}{v^{S}}\right)^{\prime},\\
&(v^{S},u^{S},\theta^{S})(-\infty)=(v^{*},u^{*},\theta^{*}),\quad(v^{S},u^{S},\theta^{S})(+\infty)=(v_{+},u_{+},\theta_{+}),\end{cases}$ where $E^{S}:=\frac{R}{\gamma-1}\theta^{S}+\frac{(u^{S})^{2}}{2}$ , and the shock speed $\sigma$ is given by $\sigma=\sqrt{-\frac{p(v_{+},\theta_{+})-p(v^{*},\theta^{*})}{v_{+}-v^{*}}}.$ Suppose that the boundary state $U_{-}\in\Omega^{+}_{sub}$ and the far-field state $U_{+}$ are connected by a boundary layer curve, a 1-rarefaction curve, a contact discontinuity curve, and a 3-Hugoniot locus, i.e., there exist intermediate states $U_{*}\in\Gamma_{trans}^{+}$ , $U_{m}$ , and $U^{*}$ such that $U_{-}\in BL(U_{*}),\quad U_{m}\in R_{1}(U_{*}),\quad U^{*}\in CD(U_{m}),\quad U^{*}\in S_{3}(U_{+}).$ Then, the asymptotic state is expected to be a superposition of a boundary layer, a 1-rarefaction, a viscous contact wave and a viscous 3-shock wave. The goal of this paper is to establish the stability of this superposition. 1.1. Main Result Now, we state the main result for the inflow problem ( 1.2 ). Theorem 1.1 . Suppose that $1<\gamma\leq 2$ . For any $U_{*}\in\Gamma_{trans}^{+}$ , fix a state $U_{m}\in\Omega_{super}^{+}$ with $U_{m}\in R_{1}(U_{*})$ . Then there exist positive constants $\delta_{0},\varepsilon_{0}$ such that the following holds. For any three states $U_{-},U^{*},U_{+}$ with $U_{-}\in BL(U_{*}),U^{*}\in CD(U_{m})$ , and $U_{+}\in S_{3}(U^{*})$ satisfying $|U_{-}-U_{*}|+|U_{m}-U^{*}|+|U^{*}-U_{+}|<\delta_{0},$ there exist a sufficiently large constant $\beta>0$ $($ depending only on the shock strength $|U^{*}-U_{+}|)$ and a sufficiently small constant $0<\varepsilon_{r}<1$ $($ depending on $\delta_{0}$ and the rarefaction strength $|U_{m}-U_{*}|)$ such that the following holds. Let $U^{R}$ be the smooth rarefaction wave with the parameter $\varepsilon_{r}$ defined in ( 1 ). If any initial data $U_{0}$ satisfies (1.19) $\displaystyle\|U_{0}-U^{*}-(U^{R}(0,\cdot)-U_{m})\|_{L^{2}(0,\beta)}+\|U_{0}-U_{+}-(U^{R}(0,\cdot)-U_{m})\|_{L^{2}(\beta,\infty)}$ $\displaystyle+\|(U_{0}-U^{R}(0,\cdot))_{x}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}<\varepsilon_{0},$ then the inflow problem ( 1.2 ) subject to the initial data $U_{0}$ admits a global-in-time solution $U(t,x)$ . Moreover, there exists a Lipschitz continuous shift function $X(t)$ such that (1.20) $\displaystyle(v-\bar{v},u-\bar{u},\theta-\bar{\theta})\in C(0,\infty;H^{1}(\sigma_{-}t,\infty)),$ $\displaystyle(u-\bar{u})_{xx}\in L^{2}(0,\infty;L^{2}(\sigma_{-}t,\infty)),\quad(\theta-\bar{\theta})_{xx}\in L^{2}(0,\infty;L^{2}(\sigma_{-}t,\infty)),$ where $\overline{U}(t,x)$ denotes the superposition of a boundary layer, a smooth 1-rarefaction, a contact wave and a viscous 3-shock wave: (1.21) $\overline{U}(t,x)=U^{BL}(x-\sigma_{-}t)+U^{R}(t,x)+U^{C}(t,x)+U^{S}(x-\sigma t-X(t)-\beta)-U_{*}-U_{m}-U^{*}.$ Furthermore, the solution asymptotically converges to the superposition of a BL solution, an inviscid 1-rarefaction, a viscous contact wave, and a viscous 3-shock wave (1.22) $\displaystyle\sup_{x\in(\sigma_{-}t,\infty)}\Big|U(t,x)-\big(U^{BL}(x-\sigma_{-}t)+U^{r}(t,x)+U^{C}(t,x)$ $\displaystyle+U^{S}(x-\sigma t-X(t)-\beta)-U_{*}-U_{m}-U^{*}\big)\Big|\to 0,$ as $t\to\infty$ , and the speed of the shift $X(t)$ converges to zero: (1.23) $|\dot{X}(t)|\to 0\,\,\text{as }t\to\infty.$ Remark 1.2 . (1) Theorem 1.1 requires the strengths of BL solution, viscous contact wave, and viscous shock wave to be small and bounded by the constant $\delta_{0}$ , which does not depend on $\varepsilon_{0}$ the smallness of the initial perturbation. However, the rarefaction strength need not be small. Unlike the barotropic Navier–Stokes system as in [ 11 , 35 ] , little is known about explicit formula of the boundary layer curve. So, it is natural to define the boundary layer curve starting from the given right state $U_{*}$ via the center-stable manifold. (2) The assumption $1<\gamma\leq 2$ is physically natural. For an ideal gas, the adiabatic exponent is determined by the number of effective molecular degrees of freedom; in particular, ordinary gases have at least three translational degrees of freedom, leading to $\gamma\leq 5/3<2$ . More generally, additional rotational or vibrational modes only decrease $\gamma$ , so the range $1<\gamma\leq 2$ covers the physically relevant regimes.
Technically, the restriction $1<\gamma\leq 2$ is only required in the proof for the monotonicity ( 5.33 ) of $v^{R}\theta^{R}$ that is used to get a sharp estimate for the diffusion term despite the large strength of a rarefaction wave. (3) We first shift the viscous shock profile by a sufficiently large constant $\beta>0$ , which is sufficiently larger than the reciprocal of shock strength. That is, we consider the initial location of shock front sufficiently far from the boundary by $O(\beta)$ . This allows us to control the discrepancy between the prescribed boundary data and the boundary values of the viscous shock profile. This, in turn, guarantees the stability of the viscous shock under sufficiently small perturbations. (4) The $L^{2}$ -norm of the initial perturbation is measured relative to the shift $\beta$ , since the viscous shock is located far from the origin by $\beta$ -scale. Moreover, since the strength of a rarefaction wave is large, while the initial perturbation is small, the approximate rarefaction $U^{R}$ is included in all the norms appearing in the initial perturbation. (5) Since the speed of shift $X(t)$ vanishes in the limit as $t\to\infty$ , the shifted shock wave would time-asymptotically belong to one-parameter family of shock waves. However, we do not ensure the final destination of shift, since we do not guarantee the existence of $\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow+\infty}X(t)$ . 1.2. Previous Results There have been extensive studies on the well-posedness of hyperbolic systems of conservation laws and viscous conservation laws. For scalar conservation laws, Bardos, le Roux, and Nédélec [ 4 ] extended the Kružkov theory [ 34 ] to initial-boundary value problems by introducing an appropriate entropy boundary condition. We also refer to [ 37 , 43 ] for further developments on boundary conditions and traces. For systems of conservation laws, Amadori [ 1 ] proved the existence of small BV entropy solutions, with the boundary condition determined by the whole-space Riemann problem, allowing for characteristic boundaries. Later, Amadori and Colombo [ 2 ] constructed the Standard Riemann Semigroup for $2\times 2$ systems, and Donadello and Marson [ 7 ] subsequently extended this construction to $N\times N$ systems with non-characteristic boundaries. More recently, Ancona, Marson, and Spinolo [ 3 ] showed the existence of small BV solutions satisfying a boundary condition consistent with the vanishing-viscosity approximation, building on the boundary-layer analyses in [ 5 , 6 ] . We next turn to viscous conservation laws. In initial-boundary value problems, boundary effects may give rise to nontrivial stationary solutions as possible asymptotic states. We refer to [ 13 , 21 , 31 , 32 , 33 , 41 , 42 ] for studies of stationary solutions for the compressible Navier–Stokes system and related systems in one and several space dimensions. In the one-dimensional inflow setting, the associated boundary Riemann problem for the corresponding Euler system, as characterized in [ 3 , 5 ] , determines the asymptotic wave pattern of the Navier–Stokes system (see also [ 35 ] ). For the isentropic Navier–Stokes (NS) system, Matsumura and Nishihara [ 36 ] established the stability of BL solutions, rarefaction waves, and their superpositions. Later, Fan, Liu, Wang, and Zhao [ 9 ] extended these stability results for boundary layer solutions (stationary solutions) and rarefaction waves to allow initial perturbations with large oscillations. For the NSF system, Huang, Li, and Shi [ 15 ] proved the stability of boundary layers, rarefaction waves, and their superpositions. Qin and Wang [ 38 ] established the stability of superpositons consisting of a nondegnerate BL solution, rarefaction waves, and a viscous contact wave. Subsequently, Qin and Wang [ 39 ] extended this result to the case of a degenerate BL solution. Regarding viscous shock waves in the NS system, Huang, Matsumura, and Shi [ 17 ] proved the stability of a superposition of a boundary layer solution and a viscous shock by employing the antiderivative method and by introducing the modified variable. However, the stability of wave patterns including a viscous shock in the inflow problem for the NSF system has remained open. In contrast to the classical antiderivative method used in earlier works, the method of $a$ -contraction with shifts provides an alternative framework for studying initial-boundary value problems for the compressible Navier–Stokes system (see, for example, [ 11 , 19 , 23 ] ). In this paper, we employ the method of $a$ -contraction with shifts to resolve the open problem on the long-time behavior toward an asymptotic pattern containing a shock wave. More importantly, our asymptotic pattern consists of a degenerate boundary layer solution, a rarefaction wave, a viscous contact wave, and a viscous shock. We emphasize that this is the most generic asymptotic pattern containing a shock. 1.3. Idea of Proof The main part of the proof is devoted to deriving the $L^{2}$ -estimates for perturbations. In the whole-space setting, the $L^{2}$ -distance provides, as in [ 29 ] , a unified framework for establishing the stability of small perturbations around a composite wave consisting of a rarefaction wave, a viscous contact wave, and a viscous shock wave. So, we employ the same $L^{2}$ -framework for the initial-boundary value problem considered in the present paper. The key ingredient of the $L^{2}$ -framework is the method of $a$ -contraction with shifts (in short, the $a$ -contraction method), which provides a robust method for controlling perturbations around viscous shock waves.
This method has been successfully applied to establish contraction properties for viscous shocks on the half-line in [ 11 , 19 , 23 ] (see also [ 8 , 10 , 12 , 20 , 22 , 24 , 25 , 26 , 27 , 28 , 29 ] for the whole-space setting). To derive the zeroth-order estimates, we employ a weighted relative entropy functional with a temporal shift to control the perturbation around the composite wave under the a priori assumption of smallness. The weight is chosen according to the viscous shock profile as defined in ( 3.2 ), while the time evolution of the weighted relative entropy provides the fundamental dissipative structure established in Lemma 5.2 . In addition, the temporal shift is defined as the average of a localized perturbation, which enables us to control the bad terms localized near the shock through the $a$ -contraction method. More precisely, the main difficulties in establishing the zeroth-order energy estimates are as follows. As the core of the $a$ -contraction method, we first maximize the relative entropy flux and then apply the change of variables $\xi\mapsto y$ defined by ( 5.27 ) to the leading-order terms associated with the modulation term (induced by the temporal shift), the diffusion terms, and the hyperbolic terms localized by the derivatives of the shock profile (or equivalently, the weight function). Under this transformation, these leading-order contributions are reduced to quantities that can be controlled by the Poincaré-type inequality stated in Lemma 2.6 . Unlike the previous works based on the $a$ -contraction method, however, the main difficulty in the present problem arises from the fact that the rarefaction wave is not assumed to have small strength. In particular, this creates a serious obstacle when reducing the diffusion term $D_{\theta_{1}}:=\kappa\int\frac{a}{v\theta}|(\theta-\bar{\theta})_{\xi}|^{2}\,d\xi$ to the favorable form $\int_{y_{0}}^{1}(y-y_{0})(1-y)|(\theta-\bar{\theta})_{y}|^{2}\,dy$ which appears in Lemma 2.6 , through the Jacobian estimates for $dy/d\xi$ established in Lemma 5.8 . Indeed, because the rarefaction wave may have large amplitude, the coefficient $1/(v\theta)$ is no longer uniformly close to $1/(v^{S}\theta^{S})$ . On the other hand, the Jacobian estimates depend only on the viscous shock profile and therefore do not directly capture the effect of the rarefaction wave. To overcome this difficulty, we observe a delicate monotonicity property of the quantity $v^{S}\theta^{S}$ , established in ( 5.33 ), which holds for $1<\gamma\leq 2$ . This property follows from the explicit representation of the entropy in ( 1.6 ), together with the fact that the rarefaction wave preserves the entropy. As a consequence, the mismatch between the diffusion coefficient and the Jacobian can still be controlled, allowing us to recover the favorable diffusion structure required for the Poincaré-type inequality. Second, another major difficulty arises in estimating the interactions among the wave components, since the boundary layer is degenerate and the rarefaction wave is allowed to have large strength.
In the present problem, we consider a degenerate boundary layer whose right state lies on the transonic curve. Unlike the non-degenerate case, such a boundary layer exhibits only algebraic decay toward the far field, as shown in Lemma 2.1 . Consequently, its interactions with the other wave components decay much more slowly and therefore require a more delicate analysis. A further difficulty stems from the large-amplitude rarefaction wave. To control its interactions with the other wave components, we introduce a smooth approximate rarefaction profile parameterized by a small parameter $\varepsilon_{r}$ , whose transition layer has thickness of order $\varepsilon_{r}^{-1}$ . This construction ensures that the derivatives of the approximate rarefaction are sufficiently small. The parameter $\varepsilon_{r}$ is then chosen appropriately so that all interaction terms involving the rarefaction wave remain sufficiently small. In this way, the approximation error of the smooth rarefaction profile and the nonlinear interaction terms can be balanced simultaneously within the stability analysis. Third, a fundamental difficulty in the analysis of the initial-boundary value problem arises from the boundary terms generated by integration by parts. For the rarefaction wave constructed in ( 2.4 ), its boundary value coincides with its left state $U_{*}$ , so this component produces no boundary mismatch. Consequently, the boundary discrepancies arise only from the viscous contact wave and the viscous shock.
For the viscous contact wave, the inflow structure implies that its boundary discrepancy decays exponentially in time, as shown in ( 5.46 ). In particular, it is bounded by the contact wave strength $\delta_{C}$ . For the viscous shock, the a priori estimate for the temporal shift implies that $|\dot{X}(t)|$ remains sufficiently small, so that the propagation speed of the shifted shock stays close to the Rankine–Hugoniot speed. Combined with the exponential decay of the viscous shock profile, this yields a small boundary discrepancy. More precisely, the discrepancy can be bounded by $e^{-c\delta_{S}\beta}$ , provided that the initial shock location $\beta$ is chosen sufficiently large compared to $\delta_{S}^{-1}$ . Finally, by applying an interpolation inequality, we show that all boundary terms can be controlled by these small boundary discrepancies together with the diffusion terms. Consequently, the boundary contributions can be absorbed into the energy estimates. 1.4. Organization of the Paper The paper is organized as follows. In Section 2 , we provide the useful quantitative properties of the boundary layer solution, rarefaction wave, viscous contact wave, and viscous shock. In Section 3 , we state the local existence result and formulate the a priori estimates. Section 4 is devoted to estimates of the interactions between the boundary layer and the elementary waves. In Section 5 and Section 6 , we establish the $L^{2}$ - and $H^{1}$ - energy estimates for the perturbation between the solutions to the inflow problem ( 1.3 ) and the superposed wave profile $\overline{U}$ , thereby completing the proof of Proposition 3.2 . 2. Preliminaries In this section, we provide several fundamental properties of the basic wave patterns, namely BL solutions, (approximate) rarefaction waves, viscous contact waves, and viscous shock waves. We also present a one-dimensional Poincaré-type inequality on any compact interval, which is a key ingredient in deriving the $L^{2}$ energy estimates localized by a viscous shock wave. 2.1. Boundary Layer Solution For given $U_{*}\in\Gamma_{trans}^{+}$ and $U_{-}\in BL(U_{*})$ , [ 44 ] showed the existence of a degenerate BL solution $U^{BL}$ connecting $U_{-}$ to $U_{*}$ . The following lemma presents the quantitative properties of the degenerate BL solution. Lemma 2.1 . [ 38 , 44 ] For given $U_{*}\in\Gamma_{trans}$ and $U_{-}\in BL(U_{*})$ , let $U^{BL}(\xi)$ be a BL solution connecting $U_{-}$ to $U_{*}$ with strength $\delta_{BL}:=|u_{-}-u_{*}|$ . Then, the following holds: (1) $v^{BL}_{\xi}>0,\,\,u^{BL}_{\xi}>0$ , and $\theta^{BL}_{\xi}<0$ . (2) There exist constants $\delta_{0}>0$ and $C>0$ such that for any $0<\delta_{BL}\leq\delta_{0}$ , (2.1) $\left|\frac{d^{n}}{d\xi^{n}}(v^{BL}-v_{*},u^{BL}-u_{*},\theta^{BL}-\theta_{*})\right|\leq\frac{(\delta_{BL})^{n+1}}{(1+\delta_{BL}\xi)^{n+1}},\quad\xi>0,\quad n=0,1,2,\cdots.$ Proof. For the proofs of (1) and (2), we refer to [ 44 ] and [ 38 ] , respectively.
∎ 2.2. Approximate Rarefaction Wave In ( 1.14 ), we constructed the smooth rarefaction wave $(v^{R},u^{R},\theta^{R})(t,\xi)$ , which approximates the rarefaction wave $(v^{r},u^{r},\theta^{r})$ connecting $(v_{*},u_{*},\theta_{*})$ to $(v_{m},u_{m},\theta_{m})$ . Since the basic properties of $(v^{R},u^{R},\theta^{R})$ follow from those of the smooth solution $w$ of the Burgers equation in ( 1 ), we first provide the following estimates on $w$ . Throughout the paper, any rarefaction strength appearing in the estimates is replaced by its maximum with $1$ if necessary, and hence is understood to be bounded below by $1$ . Lemma 2.2 . [ 16 ] Suppose that $w_{-}<w_{+}$ . Then, ( 1 ) admits a unique smooth solution $w(t,x)$ satisfying the following properties: (1) $w_{-}\leq w(t,x)<w_{+},~w_{x}(t,x)>0$ . (2) For any $p$ $(1\leq p\leq\infty)$ , there exists a constant $C_{pq}$ such that $\displaystyle\|w_{x}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leq C_{pq}\min\big\{\delta_{r}\varepsilon_{r}^{1-1/p},~\delta_{r}^{1/p}t^{-1+1/p}\big\},$ $\displaystyle\|w_{xx}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leq C_{pq}\min\big\{\delta_{r}\varepsilon_{r}^{2-1/p},~\delta_{r}^{1/q}\varepsilon_{r}^{1-1/p+1/q}t^{-1+1/q}\big\}.$ (3) If $x>w_{+}t$ , then $\displaystyle|w(t,x)-w_{+}|\leq C\delta_{r}e^{-c\varepsilon_{r}\left|x-w_{+}t\right|},$ $\displaystyle|w_{x}(t,x)|\leq C\varepsilon_{r}\delta_{r}e^{-c\varepsilon_{r}\left|x-w_{+}t\right|}.$ (4) As $t\to\infty$ , we have $\sup\limits_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left|w(t,x)-w^{r}\left(\frac{x}{t}\right)\right|\rightarrow 0,$ where $w^{r}(x/t)$ denotes the inviscid rarefaction wave of the Burgers equation given by $w^{r}\left(\frac{x}{t}\right)=\begin{cases}w_{-}\,\,&\text{if }x<w_{-}t,\\
\frac{x}{t}\,\,&\text{if }w_{-}t<x<w_{+}t,\\
w_{+}\,\,&\text{if }x>w_{+}t.\end{cases}$ Proof. Since (1), (2), and (4) were proved in [ 16 ] , For completeness, we provide a proof only for property (3). By the method of characteristics, the solution to the Burgers equation ( 1 ) can be represented as $\displaystyle w(t,x)=w\left(x_{0}(t,x)\right),$ where $x_{0}(t,x)$ is determined by $x=x_{0}(t,x)+w\left(x_{0}(t,x)\right)t.$ It is easy to see that (2.2) $\displaystyle\frac{\partial x_{0}(t,x)}{\partial x}=\frac{1}{1+w^{\prime}_{0}(x_{0})t},\quad\text{and}\quad w_{x}=\frac{w_{0}^{\prime}(x_{0})}{1+w_{0}^{\prime}(x_{0})t}.$ It follows from $\eqref{eq:SBurgers}_{2}$ that (2.3) $\displaystyle w_{0}^{\prime}(x)=\begin{cases}0,&x<0,\\
C_{q}\delta_{r}\varepsilon_{r}(\varepsilon_{r}x)^{q}e^{-\varepsilon_{r}x},&x>0.\end{cases}$ Combining ( 2.2 ) and ( 2.3 ), we obtain the second estimate in property (3). For $x\geq 0$ , it follows from $\eqref{eq:SBurgers}_{2}$ that $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &|w(t,x)-w_{+}|=|w\left(x_{0}(t,x)\right)-w_{+}|=C_{q}(w_{+}-w_{-})\int^{\infty}_{\varepsilon_{r}x_{0}(t,x)}y^{q}e^{-y}\,dy\\
&\quad=C_{q}\delta_{r}\int^{\infty}_{\varepsilon_{r}\big(x-w\left(x_{0}(t,x)\right)t\big)}y^{q}e^{-y}\,dy\leq C_{q}\delta_{r}\int^{\infty}_{\varepsilon_{r}\left(x-w_{+}t\right)}y^{q}e^{-y}\,dy\leq C_{q}\delta_{r}e^{-c\varepsilon_{r}\left(x-w_{+}t\right)},\end{aligned}$ which completes the proof.
∎ We now present the properties of approximate rarefaction wave $U^{R}$ defined in ( 1.13 ). It follows from ( 1.13 ) 1 that $|U_{\xi\xi}^{R}|\leq C(|w_{\xi}|^{2}+|w_{\xi\xi}|)$ for some positive constant $C>0$ . This estimate, together with Lemma 2.2 , yields the following Lemma. Lemma 2.3 . [ 38 ] For any $U_{m}\in R_{1}(U_{*})$ , let $U^{R}(t,\xi)$ be the approximate 1-rarefaction connecting $U_{*}$ and $U_{m}$ , defined in ( 1.14 ) with a parameter $0<\varepsilon_{r}\leq 1$ . We denote its strength by $\delta_{R}:=|u_{*}-u_{m}|\sim|v_{*}-v_{m}|\sim|\theta_{*}-\theta_{m}|$ . Then, $U^{R}(t,\xi)$ satisfies (2.4) $\begin{cases}&v^{R}_{t}-\sigma_{-}v^{R}_{\xi}-u^{R}_{\xi}=0,\quad t>0,\,\,\xi>0,\\
&u^{R}_{t}-\sigma_{-}u^{R}_{\xi}+p^{R}_{\xi}=0,\\
&\frac{R}{\gamma-1}(\theta^{R}_{t}-\sigma_{-}\theta^{R}_{\xi})+p^{R}u^{R}_{\xi}=0,\end{cases}$ where $p^{R}:=p(v^{R},\theta^{R})$ , and $(v^{R},u^{R},\theta^{R})(t,0)=(v_{*},u_{*},\theta_{*}),\quad(v^{R},u^{R},\theta^{R})(t,+\infty)=(v_{m},u_{m},\theta_{m}).$ Moreover, the following estimates hold: (1) $v^{R}_{\xi}>0,\,\,u^{R}_{\xi}>0,\,\,\theta^{R}_{\xi}<0$ , for any $t>0$ and $\xi>0$ . (2) For any $p$ $(1\leq p\leq\infty)$ , there exists a constant $C_{pq}$ such that for any $t\geq 0$ , (2.5) $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\|(v^{R}_{\xi},u^{R}_{\xi},\theta^{R}_{\xi})(t)\|_{L^{p}}\leq C_{pq}\min\big\{\delta_{R}\varepsilon_{r}^{1-1/p},~(\delta_{R})^{1/p}(1+t)^{-1+1/p}\big\},\\
&\|(v^{R}_{\xi\xi},u^{R}_{\xi\xi},\theta^{R}_{\xi\xi})(t)\|_{L^{p}}\leq C_{pq}\min\big\{\delta_{R}\varepsilon_{r}^{2-1/p},~\big((\delta_{R})^{1/p}+(\delta_{R})^{1/q}\big)(1+t)^{-1+1/q}\big\},\end{aligned}$ (3) If $\xi+\sigma_{-}t\geq\lambda_{1}(v_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)$ , then $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\left|(v^{R},u^{R},\theta^{R})(t,\xi)-(v_{m},u_{m},\theta_{m})\right|\leq C\delta_{R}e^{-c\varepsilon_{r}\left|\left(\xi+\sigma_{-}t-\lambda_{1}(v_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)\right)\right|},\\
&\left|(v^{R}_{\xi},u^{R}_{\xi},\theta^{R}_{\xi})\right|\leq C\varepsilon_{r}\delta_{R}e^{-c\varepsilon_{r}\left|\left(\xi+\sigma_{-}t-\lambda_{1}(v_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)\right)\right|}.\end{aligned}$ (4) $\sup\limits_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}\big|(v^{R},u^{R},\theta^{R})(t,\xi)-(v^{r},u^{r},\theta^{r})\big(\frac{\xi}{1+t}\big)\big|\rightarrow 0$ , as $t\rightarrow\infty$ . 2.3. Viscous Contact Wave Next, we consider $U^{*}\in CD(U_{m})$ , where $CD(U_{m})$ is the 2-contact discontinuity curve defined in ( 1.15 ). Let $U^{C}$ be the viscous contact wave connecting $U^{*}$ and $U_{m}$ , defined in ( 1.17 ).
Then, $(v^{C},u^{C},\theta^{C})(t,\xi)$ satisfies (2.6) $\begin{cases}&v^{C}_{t}-\sigma_{-}v^{C}_{\xi}-u^{C}_{\xi}=0,\quad t>0,\quad\xi>0,\\
&u^{C}_{t}-\sigma_{-}u^{C}_{\xi}+p(v^{C},\theta^{C})_{\xi}=\left(\mu\frac{u^{C}_{\xi}}{v^{C}}\right)_{\xi}+Q_{1}^{C},\\
&\frac{R}{\gamma-1}\big(\theta^{C}_{t}-\sigma_{-}\theta_{\xi}^{C}\big)+p(v^{C},\theta^{C})u_{\xi}^{C}=\left(\kappa\frac{\theta^{C}_{\xi}}{v^{C}}\right)_{\xi}+\left(\mu\frac{(u^{C}_{\xi})^{2}}{v^{C}}\right)+Q_{2}^{C},\end{cases}$ where $Q_{1}^{C}$ and $Q_{2}^{C}$ are defined by (2.7) $Q_{1}^{C}:=\big(u_{t}^{C}-\sigma_{-}u_{\xi}^{C}\big)-\left(\mu\frac{u^{C}_{\xi}}{v^{C}}\right)_{\xi},\quad Q_{2}^{C}:=-\left(\mu\frac{(u^{C}_{\xi})^{2}}{v^{C}}\right).$ The following Lemma provides the basic properties of the viscous contact wave. Lemma 2.4 . [ 18 ] For any $U^{*}\in CD(U_{m})$ , let $U^{C}(t,\xi)$ be the viscous contact wave connecting $U^{*}$ and $U_{m}$ defined in ( 1.17 ), with strength $\delta_{C}:=|v^{*}-v_{m}|\sim|\theta^{*}-\theta_{m}|$ . Then, we have the following: (1) $v^{C}$ and $\theta^{C}$ are monotone. More precisely, we have $v^{C}_{\xi}>0,\,\,\text{and }\,\theta^{C}_{\xi}>0,\,\,\text{or }\,v^{C}_{\xi}<0,\,\,\text{and }\,\theta^{C}_{\xi}<0.$ (2) There exists a constant $C_{1}>0$ such that the following estimates hold: (2.8) $\displaystyle(v^{C}-v_{m},u^{C}-u_{m},\theta^{C}-\theta_{m})=O(1)\delta_{C}e^{-\frac{C_{1}(\xi+\sigma_{-}t)^{2}}{1+t}},\quad\forall\,0<\xi<-\sigma_{-}t,$ $\displaystyle(v^{C}-v^{*},u^{C}-u^{*},\theta^{C}-\theta^{*})=O(1)\delta_{C}e^{-\frac{C_{1}(\xi+\sigma_{-}t)^{2}}{1+t}},\quad\forall\,\xi>-\sigma_{-}t,$ $\displaystyle(\partial_{\xi}^{n}v^{C},\partial_{\xi}^{n}\theta^{C})(t,\xi)=O(1)\delta_{C}(1+t)^{-\frac{n}{2}}e^{-\frac{C_{1}(\xi+\sigma_{-}t)^{2}}{1+t}},\quad\forall(t,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+},\quad n=1,2,\cdots$ $\displaystyle\partial_{\xi}^{n}u^{C}(t,\xi)=O(1)\delta_{C}(1+t)^{-\frac{1+n}{2}}e^{-\frac{C_{1}(\xi+\sigma_{-}t)^{2}}{1+t}}\quad\forall\,(t,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+},\quad n=1,2,\cdots.$ By Lemma 2.4 , the error terms $Q_{1}^{C}$ and $Q_{2}^{C}$ in ( 2.7 ) can be estimated as follows: (2.9) $\displaystyle Q_{1}^{C}=O(1)\delta_{C}(1+t)^{-\frac{3}{2}}e^{-\frac{2C_{1}(\xi+\sigma_{-}t)^{2}}{1+t}},\quad Q_{2}^{C}=O(1)\delta_{C}(1+t)^{-2}e^{-\frac{2C_{1}(\xi+\sigma_{-}t)^{2}}{1+t}}.$ 2.4. Viscous Shock Wave Finally, we consider a state $U_{+}\in S_{3}(U^{*})$ , i.e., $U^{*}$ and $U_{+}$ satisfy the Rankine-Hugoniot conditions: (2.10) $\begin{cases}&-\sigma(v_{+}-v^{*})-(u_{+}-u^{*})=0,\\
&-\sigma(u_{+}-u^{*})+p(v_{+},\theta_{+})-p(v^{*},\theta^{*})=0,\\
&-\sigma(E_{+}-E^{*})+\big(p(v_{+},\theta_{+})u_{+}-p(v^{*},\theta^{*})u^{*}\big)=0,\end{cases}$ and the entropy condition $v^{*}<v_{+},u^{*}>u_{+}$ , and $\theta^{*}>\theta_{+}$ . It is well-known that there exists a viscous 3-shock profile $U^{S}(\zeta)=U^{S}(x-\sigma t)=U^{S}(\xi-(\sigma-\sigma_{-})t)$ satisfying ( 1.18 ), provided that the shock strength is sufficiently small. Then, the following lemma provides the quantitative estimates for viscous weak shock waves. Lemma 2.5 . [ 29 ] For any fixed state $U^{*}$ , there exists a positive constant $\delta_{0}$ such that the following holds. For any end state $U_{+}$ such that $U_{+}\in S_{3}(U^{*})$ with $|U_{+}-U^{*}|\leq\delta_{0}$ , there exists a unique solution $U^{S}$ to ( 1.18 ) with $U^{S}(0)=\frac{U^{*}+U_{+}}{2}$ . Let $\delta_{S}$ be the shock strength defined by $\delta_{S}:=|u_{+}-u^{*}|\sim|v_{+}-v^{*}|\sim|\theta-\theta^{*}|$ . Then, there exists a positive constant $C>0$ such that the following estimates hold: (2.11) $\displaystyle v_{\zeta}^{S}>0,\quad u_{\zeta}^{S}<0,\quad\theta_{\zeta}^{S}<0,\quad\forall\zeta\in\mathbb{R},$ $\displaystyle|(v^{S}(\zeta)-v^{*},u^{S}(\zeta)-u^{*},\theta^{S}(\zeta)-\theta^{*})|\leq C\delta_{S}e^{-C\delta_{S}|\zeta|},\quad\zeta<0,$ $\displaystyle|(v^{S}(\zeta)-v_{+},u^{S}(\zeta)-u_{+},\theta^{S}(\zeta)-\theta_{+})|\leq C\delta_{S}e^{-C\delta_{S}|\zeta|},\quad\zeta>0,$ $\displaystyle|(v_{\zeta}^{S},u_{\zeta}^{S},\theta_{\zeta}^{S})|\leq C\delta_{S}^{2}e^{-C\delta_{S}|\zeta|},\quad\forall\zeta\in\mathbb{R},$ $\displaystyle|(v_{\zeta\zeta}^{S},u_{\zeta\zeta}^{S},\theta_{\zeta\zeta}^{S})|\leq C\delta_{S}|(v_{\zeta}^{S},u_{\zeta}^{S},\theta_{\zeta}^{S})|.$ In particular, $|v_{\zeta}^{S}|\sim|u_{\zeta}^{S}|\sim|\theta_{\zeta}^{S}|$ , and for all $\zeta\in\mathbb{R}$ , (2.12) $\displaystyle\left|v^{S}_{\zeta}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{*}}u^{S}_{\zeta}\right|\leq C\delta_{S}|u^{S}_{\zeta}|,\quad\text{and}\quad\left|\theta^{S}_{\zeta}-\frac{(\gamma-1)p^{*}}{R\sigma^{*}}u^{S}_{\zeta}\right|\leq C\delta_{S}|u^{S}_{\zeta}|,$ where $p^{*}:=p(v^{*},\theta^{*}),\quad\sigma^{*}:=\sqrt{\frac{\gamma p^{*}}{v^{*}}}=\frac{\sqrt{\gamma R\theta^{*}}}{v^{*}},$ which satisfies $\displaystyle|\sigma-\sigma^{*}|\leq C\delta_{S}.$ 2.5. Poincaré-type Inequality We present a Poincaré-type inequality on any compact subset of $\mathbb{R}$ , where the optimal constant $1/2$ is independent of the size of the domain. Lemma 2.6 . [ 19 ] For any $c<d$ and function $f:[c,d]\to\mathbb{R}$ satisfying $\int_{c}^{d}(y-c)(d-y)|f^{\prime}(y)|^{2}\,dy<\infty$ , $\int_{c}^{d}\left|f(y)-\frac{1}{d-c}\int_{c}^{d}f(y)\,dy\right|^{2}\,dy\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{c}^{d}(y-c)(d-y)|f^{\prime}(y)|^{2}\,dy.$ 3. A Priori Estimates and Proof of Main Theorem 3.1. Local Existence In this subsection, we state a local-in-time existence result for the inflow problem ( 1.2 ). Since the proof is based on a standard iteration argument (see, for example, [ 40 ] ), we omit the details. Proposition 3.1 . For any constant $\beta>0$ , let $\underline{v},\underline{u}$ and $\underline{\theta}$ be smooth functions such that $(\underline{v}(x),\underline{u}(x),\underline{\theta}(x))=(v_{+},u_{+},\theta_{+}),\quad\mbox{for}\quad x\geq\beta,\quad\underline{v}(0)>0\quad\text{and}\quad\underline{\theta}(0)>0.$ For any constants $M_{0}$ , $M_{1}$ , $\underline{\kappa}_{0}$ , $\overline{\kappa}_{0}$ , $\underline{\kappa}_{1}$ , and $\overline{\kappa}_{1}$ with $0<M_{0}<M_{1}$ and $0<\underline{\kappa}_{1}<\underline{\kappa}_{0}<\overline{\kappa}_{0}<\overline{\kappa}_{1}$ , there exists a constant $T_{0}>0$ such that if $\displaystyle\|(v_{0}-\underline{v},u_{0}-\underline{u},\theta_{0}-\underline{\theta})\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}\leq M_{0},$ $\displaystyle 0<\underline{\kappa}_{0}\leq v_{0}(x),\theta_{0}(x)\leq\overline{\kappa}_{0},\quad x\in\mathbb{R}_{+},$ the inflow problem ( 1.2 ) admits a unique solution $(v,u,\theta)$ on $[0,T_{0}]$ such that $\displaystyle v-\underline{v}\in C([0,T_{0}];H^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})),\quad(u-\underline{u},\theta-\underline{\theta})\in C([0,T_{0}];H^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+}))\cap L^{2}(0,T_{0};H^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})),$ and $\|(v-\underline{v},u-\underline{u},\theta-\underline{\theta})\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T_{0};H^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+}))}\leq M_{1}.$ Moreover, for the inflow problem, we have $\underline{\kappa}_{1}\leq v(t,x)\leq\overline{\kappa}_{1},\quad\underline{\kappa}_{1}\leq\theta(t,x)\leq\overline{\kappa}_{1},\quad\forall t>0,\,\,\forall x\in\mathbb{R}_{+},$ and $v(t,0)=v_{-}>0,\quad u(t,0)=u_{-}>0\,\,,\quad\theta(t,0)=\theta_{-}>0,\quad\forall t>0.$ 3.2. Construction of the Shift and the Weight Function In order to establish the stability of the viscous shock, we need to consider a suitable shift of the viscous shock wave. To this end, we construct the shift function $X(t)$ as the solution to the following ODE: (3.1) $\begin{cases}&\dot{X}(t)=-\frac{M}{\delta_{S}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}a\left((u^{S})_{\xi}(u-\bar{u})+\frac{(v^{S})_{\xi}p^{*}}{v^{*}}(v-\bar{v})+\frac{R}{\gamma-1}\frac{(\theta^{S})_{\xi}}{\theta^{*}}(\theta-\bar{\theta})\right)\,d\xi\right),\\
&X(0)=0.\end{cases}$ where $(v,u,\theta)$ is a solution to ( 1.3 ), $a$ is the weight function defined in ( 3.2 ), and $M>0$ is a constant to be chosen later. By the argument of [ 29 , Lemma 3.2] , the initial value problem ( 3.1 ) admits a unique Lipschitz continuous solution on $[0,T]$ under the assumptions of Proposition 3.2 . We then define the weight function $a$ by (3.2) $a=a(t,\xi):=1+\frac{(u^{*}-u^{S}(\xi-(\sigma-\sigma_{-})t-X(t)-\beta))}{\sqrt{\delta_{S}}}.$ Since $u^{S}$ is decreasing, we find that $a$ is increasing. Moreover, observe that $1\leq a\leq 1+\sqrt{\delta_{S}},$ and (3.3) $\displaystyle\partial_{\xi}a(t,\xi)=-\frac{u^{S}_{\xi}}{\sqrt{\delta_{S}}}>0.$ We remark that the weight function $a$ will play a crucial role in the $L^{2}$ estimates by producing the new good term $G^{new}$ in ( 5.26 ). 3.3. A Priori Estimates We now present the a priori estimates, which are a key ingredient in proving Theorem 1.1 . For notational convenience, we omit the arguments if there is no confusion: $U^{BL}=U^{BL}(\xi),\quad U^{R}=U^{R}(t,\xi),\quad U^{C}=U^{C}(t,\xi),\quad U^{S}=U^{S}(\xi-(\sigma-\sigma_{-})t-X(t)-\beta).$ Proposition 3.2 . Suppose that $1<\gamma\leq 2$ . For any $U_{*}\in\Gamma_{trans}^{+}$ , fix a state $U_{m}\in\Omega_{super}^{+}$ with $U_{m}\in R_{1}(U_{*})$ . Then there exist positive constants $\delta_{0},\varepsilon,c$ , and $C_{0}$ such that the following holds. For any three states $U_{-},U^{*},U_{+}$ with $U_{-}\in BL(U_{*}),U^{*}\in CD(U_{m})$ , and $U_{+}\in S_{3}(U^{*})$ satisfying $|U_{-}-U_{*}|+|U_{m}-U^{*}|+|U^{*}-U_{+}|<\delta_{0},$ there exist a sufficiently large constant $\beta>0$ $($ depending only on the shock strength $|U^{*}-U_{+}|)$ and a sufficiently small constant $0<\varepsilon_{r}<1$ $($ depending on $\delta_{0}$ and the rarefaction strength $|U_{m}-U_{*}|)$ such that the following holds. Let $(v,u,\theta)$ be the solution to ( 1.3 )-( 1.4 ) on $[0,T]$ for some $T>0$ , and let $(\bar{v},\bar{u},\bar{\theta})$ be the superposition defined in ( 1.21 ).
Let $X(t)$ be the shift function defined in ( 3.1 ), and let $a$ be the weight function defined in ( 3.2 ). Assume that $\displaystyle v-\bar{v}\in C([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})),\quad(u-\bar{u},\theta-\bar{\theta})\in C([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+}))\cap L^{2}(0,T;H^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})),$ and (3.4) $\|(v-\bar{v},u-\bar{u},\theta-\bar{\theta})\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+}))}\leq\varepsilon.$ Then, the following estimate holds: (3.5) $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|(v-\bar{v},u-\bar{u},\theta-\bar{\theta})(t,\cdot)\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\big(\delta_{S}|\dot{X}(t)|^{2}+G^{BL}(U)+G^{R}(U)+G^{S}(U)\big)\,dt\\
&\qquad+\int_{0}^{T}\big(D_{v_{1}}(U)+D_{u_{1}}(U)+D_{u_{2}}(U)+D_{\theta_{1}}(U)+D_{\theta_{2}}(U)\big)\,dt\\
&\quad\leq C_{0}\|(v-\bar{v},u-\bar{u},\theta-\bar{\theta})(0,\cdot)\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}^{2}+C_{0}\delta_{0}^{1/40}+C_{0}e^{-c\delta_{S}\beta},\end{aligned}$ where $\displaystyle G^{BL}(U):=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|u^{BL}_{\xi}|\,|(v-\bar{v},\theta-\bar{\theta})|^{2}\,d\xi,\qquad G^{R}(U):=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|u^{R}_{\xi}|\,|(v-\bar{v},\theta-\bar{\theta})|^{2}\,d\xi,$ $\displaystyle G^{S}(U):=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|v^{S}_{\xi}|\,|(v-\bar{v},u-\bar{u},\theta-\bar{\theta})|^{2}\,d\xi,$ $\displaystyle D_{v_{1}}(U):=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|(v-\bar{v})_{\xi}|^{2}d\xi,\,\,\,D_{u_{1}}(U):=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|(u-\bar{u})_{\xi}|^{2}d\xi,\,\,\,D_{u_{2}}(U):=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|(u-\bar{u})_{\xi\xi}|^{2}d\xi,$ $\displaystyle D_{\theta_{1}}(U):=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|(\theta-\bar{\theta})_{\xi}|^{2}d\xi,\,\,\,D_{\theta_{2}}(U):=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|(\theta-\bar{\theta})_{\xi\xi}|^{2}\,d\xi.$ 3.4. Proof of Theorem 1.1 Based on Proposition 3.1 and Proposition 3.2 , we use a continuity argument to prove the global existence result ( 1.20 ) for the inflow problem. The detailed proof of ( 1.20 ) is deferred to the Appendix C . In addition, the time-asymptotic stability ( 1.22 ) can be proved by showing that $\|(v-\bar{v},u-\bar{u},\theta-\bar{\theta})_{\xi}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}^{2}\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}_{+}).$ Since the proof of ( 1.22 ) is standard and similar to that in [ 11 ] , we omit the details. Finally, ( 1.23 ) directly follows from ( 3.1 ) and ( 1.22 ). 3.5. Notations The following sections are devoted to proving Proposition 3.2 . In what follows, we omit the spatial domain $\mathbb{R}_{+}$ whenever there is no confusion. For example, we write $L^{p}$ and $H^{1}$ for $L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+})$ and $H^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})$ , respectively. Moreover, we introduce the following notations: (3.6) $\displaystyle(v^{BL},u^{BL},\theta^{BL})=:(v_{1},u_{1},\theta_{1}),\quad(v^{R},u^{R},\theta^{R})=:(v_{2},u_{2},\theta_{2})$ $\displaystyle(v^{C},u^{C},\theta^{C})=:(v_{3},u_{3},\theta_{3}),\quad(v^{S},u^{S},\theta^{S})=:(v_{4},u_{4},\theta_{4}),$ and $(v-\bar{v},u-\bar{u},\theta-\bar{\theta}):=(\phi,\psi,\chi).$ Moreover, the constant $C>0$ may change line to line, which is independent of $\delta_{0},\varepsilon_{r},\varepsilon,\beta$ , and time $T>0$ . Unless otherwise stated, $C$ may depend on $\delta_{R}$ ; however, in ( 4.2 ), ( 4.12 ), ( 4.13 ), ( 4.15 ), and ( 4.23 ), it is independent of $\delta_{R}$ . 4. Wave Interaction Estimates In this section, we provide several useful estimates on the wave interactions. To this end, we define $J_{i}$ , $i=1,\cdots,6$ , by (4.1) $\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{aligned} &J_{1}:=|v_{\xi}^{BL}||v^{R}-v_{*}|+|v_{\xi}^{R}||v^{BL}-v_{*}|,&&J_{2}:=|v_{\xi}^{BL}||v^{C}-v_{m}|+|v_{\xi}^{C}||v^{BL}-v_{*}|,\\
&J_{3}:=|v_{\xi}^{BL}||v^{S}-v^{*}|+|v_{\xi}^{S}||v^{BL}-v_{*}|,&&J_{4}:=|v_{\xi}^{R}||v^{C}-v_{m}|+|v_{\xi}^{C}||v^{R}-v_{m}|,\\
&J_{5}:=|v_{\xi}^{R}||v^{S}-v^{*}|+|v_{\xi}^{S}||v^{R}-v_{m}|,&&J_{6}:=|v_{\xi}^{C}||v^{S}-v^{*}|+|v_{\xi}^{S}||v^{C}-v^{*}|.\end{aligned}\end{split}$ 4.1. $L^{1}$ Interaction Estimates First, we provide the $L^{1}$ wave-interaction estimates. We remark that the estimates in ( 4.2 ) of Lemma 4.1 , as well as those in ( 4.12 ), and ( 4.13 ) of Lemma 4.2 remain valid even when the strength of the boundary layer solution is large. Lemma 4.1 . Under the assumptions of Proposition 3.2 , there exist positive constants $c$ and $C$ such that the following estimates hold: (4.2) $\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{aligned} &\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{1}\,d\xi\leq C\left(\frac{(\varepsilon_{r}\delta_{R})^{1/8}}{(1+t)^{7/8}}\ln(1+\delta_{BL}t)+\frac{\delta_{BL}\delta_{R}}{1+\delta_{BL}t}\right),\\
&\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{2}\,d\xi\leq C\delta_{BL}\delta_{C}\left(e^{-ct}+\frac{1}{1+\delta_{BL}t}\right),\\
&\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{3}\,d\xi\leq C\delta_{BL}\delta_{S}\left(e^{-c\delta_{S}t}+\frac{1}{1+\delta_{BL}t}\right),\\
&\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{4}\,d\xi\leq C\delta_{R}\delta_{C}e^{-ct}+C\delta_{C}\frac{\delta_{R}}{\varepsilon_{r}}e^{-c\varepsilon_{r}t},\\
&\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{5}\,d\xi\leq C\delta_{R}\delta_{S}(\varepsilon_{r}+\delta_{S})e^{-c\delta_{S}t}+C\delta_{R}\delta_{S}\frac{(\varepsilon_{r}+\delta_{S})}{\varepsilon_{r}}e^{-c\varepsilon_{r}t},\\
&\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{6}\,d\xi\leq C\delta_{C}\delta_{S}\left(e^{-c\delta_{S}t}+e^{-ct}\right).\end{aligned}\end{split}$ Consequently, we have (4.3) $\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{6}\|J_{i}\|_{L^{1}}^{4/3}\,d\tau\leq C\delta_{0}+C(\varepsilon_{r}\delta_{R})^{1/6}+C\delta_{0}\left(\frac{\delta_{R}}{\varepsilon_{r}}\right)^{4/3}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{r}}.$ Proof. We estimate $J_{1}$ following the idea of [ 36 ] . First, it follows from Lemma 2.1 and Lemma 2.3 that $v_{\xi}^{BL}(v^{R}-v_{*})>0$ , and $v_{\xi}^{R}(v_{*}-v^{BL})>0$ .
Therefore, by integration by parts, together with the fact that $v^{R}(t,0)=v_{*}=v^{BL}(\infty)$ , we obtain $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{1}\,d\xi&=\left(\int_{0}^{t}+\int_{t}^{\infty}\right)\left(v^{BL}_{\xi}(v^{R}-v_{*})+v^{R}_{\xi}(v_{*}-v^{BL})\right)d\xi\\
&=\Bigg(\left[(v^{BL}-v_{*})(v^{R}-v_{*})\right]^{\xi=t}_{\xi=0}-2\int_{0}^{t}v^{R}_{\xi}(v^{BL}-v_{*})\,d\xi\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt+\left[(v^{R}-v_{*})(v_{*}-v^{BL})\right]_{\xi=t}^{\xi=\infty}+2\int_{t}^{\infty}v^{BL}_{\xi}(v^{R}-v_{*})\,d\xi\Bigg)\\
&\leq 2\int_{0}^{t}v^{R}_{\xi}(v_{*}-v^{BL})\,d\xi+2\int_{t}^{\infty}v^{BL}_{\xi}(v^{R}-v_{*})\,d\xi.\end{aligned}$ Here, we used $(v^{BL}(\xi)-v_{*})(v^{R}(t,\xi)-v_{*})<0$ for all $t,\xi>0$ . We observe from ( 2.1 ) and ( 2.5 ) that, for all $t,\xi>0$ , (4.4) $\displaystyle|v^{R}-v_{*}|\leq C\delta_{R},\quad|v^{R}_{\xi}|\leq\|v^{R}_{\xi}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{1/8}\|v^{R}_{\xi}\|^{7/8}_{L^{\infty}}\leq\frac{C(\varepsilon_{r}\delta_{R})^{1/8}}{(1+t)^{7/8}},$ $\displaystyle|v^{BL}-v_{*}|\leq\frac{C\delta_{BL}}{1+\delta_{BL}\xi},\quad|v^{BL}_{\xi}|\leq\frac{C\delta^{2}_{BL}}{(1+\delta_{BL}\xi)^{2}}.$ Therefore, by ( 4.4 ), we obtain $\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{1}\,d\xi\leq$ $\displaystyle C\int_{0}^{t}v^{R}_{\xi}(v_{*}-v^{BL})\,d\xi+C\int_{t}^{\infty}v^{BL}_{\xi}(v^{R}-v_{*})\,d\xi$ $\displaystyle\leq$ $\displaystyle\frac{C(\varepsilon_{r}\delta_{R})^{1/8}\delta_{BL}}{(1+t)^{7/8}}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{1+\delta_{BL}\xi}\,d\xi+C\delta^{2}_{BL}\delta_{R}\int_{t}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(1+\delta_{BL}\xi)^{2}}\,d\xi$ $\displaystyle=$ $\displaystyle\frac{C(\varepsilon_{r}\delta_{R})^{1/8}}{(1+t)^{7/8}}\ln(1+\delta_{BL}t)+\frac{C\delta_{BL}\delta_{R}}{1+\delta_{BL}t}.$ For $J_{2}$ , we assume that $v^{C}$ is monotone increasing. Then, we have $\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{2}\,d\xi=$ $\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\big(v_{\xi}^{BL}(v^{C}-v_{m})+v_{\xi}^{C}(v_{*}-v^{BL}))\,d\xi$ $\displaystyle=$ $\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{-\frac{\sigma_{-}t}{2}}+\int_{-\frac{\sigma_{-}t}{2}}^{\infty}\right)\big(v_{\xi}^{BL}(v^{C}-v_{m})+v_{\xi}^{C}(v_{*}-v^{BL}))\,d\xi$ $\displaystyle=$ $\displaystyle(v^{BL}-v_{*})(v^{C}-v_{m})\Big|_{0}^{-\frac{\sigma_{-}t}{2}}-2\int_{0}^{-\frac{\sigma_{-}t}{2}}v_{\xi}^{C}(v^{BL}-v_{*})\,d\xi$ $\displaystyle+(v^{C}-v_{m})(v_{*}-v^{BL})\Big|_{-\frac{\sigma_{-}t}{2}}^{\infty}+2\int_{-\frac{\sigma_{-}t}{2}}^{\infty}v_{\xi}^{BL}(v^{C}-v_{m})\,d\xi.$ Using $(v^{BL}-v_{*})(v^{C}-v_{m})<0$ , Lemma 2.1 and Lemma 2.4 , we have $\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{2}\,d\xi\leq C\delta_{BL}|v^{C}-v_{m}|\Big|_{\xi=0}+2\int_{0}^{-\frac{\sigma_{-}t}{2}}v_{\xi}^{C}(v_{*}-v^{BL})\,d\xi+2\int_{-\frac{\sigma_{-}t}{2}}^{\infty}v_{\xi}^{BL}(v^{C}-v_{m})\,d\xi$ $\displaystyle\quad\leq C\delta_{BL}\delta_{C}e^{-\frac{C_{1}(\sigma_{-}t)^{2}}{1+t}}+C\frac{\delta_{BL}\delta_{C}}{\sqrt{1+t}}\int_{0}^{-\frac{\sigma_{-}t}{2}}e^{-\frac{C_{1}(\xi+\sigma_{-}t)^{2}}{1+t}}\,d\xi+C\delta_{BL}^{2}\delta_{C}\int_{-\frac{\sigma_{-}t}{2}}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(1+\delta_{BL}\xi)^{2}}\,d\xi$ $\displaystyle\quad\leq C\delta_{BL}\delta_{C}\left(e^{-{Ct}}+\frac{1}{1+\delta_{BL}t}\right).$ The case where $v^{C}$ is decreasing can be treated similarly. The estimate for $J_{3}$ follows by the same argument as in [ 11 , Lemma 4.1] ; hence we omit the details. Next, we estimate the term $J_{4}$ . We first decompose $J_{4}$ into two terms as follows: $\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{4}\,d\xi=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|v_{\xi}^{R}||v^{C}-v_{m}|+|v_{\xi}^{C}||v^{R}-v_{m}|\,d\xi$ $\displaystyle\,\,=\left(\int_{0\leq\xi\leq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)-\sigma_{-}t}+\int_{\xi\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)-\sigma_{-}t}\right)|v_{\xi}^{R}||v^{C}-v_{m}|+|v_{\xi}^{C}||v^{R}-v_{m}|d\xi.$ For $\xi+\sigma_{-}t\leq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)$ , it follows from Lemma 2.4 that (4.5) $\displaystyle|(v^{C}-v_{m},u^{C}-u_{m},\theta^{C}-\theta_{m})|+|v^{C}_{\xi}|\leq C\delta_{C}e^{-\frac{C_{1}|\xi+\sigma_{-}t|^{2}}{1+t}}\leq C\delta_{C}e^{-\frac{C_{1}|\xi+\sigma_{-}t|^{2}}{2(1+t)}}e^{-Ct}.$ Combining this with Lemma 2.3 , we obtain (4.6) $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\int_{0\leq\xi\leq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)-\sigma_{-}t}\left(|v_{\xi}^{R}||v^{C}-v_{m}|+|v_{\xi}^{C}||v^{R}-v_{m}|\right)\,d\xi\\
&\quad\leq C\left(\varepsilon_{r}\delta_{R}+\delta_{R}\right)\delta_{C}\int_{0\leq\xi\leq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)-\sigma_{-}t}e^{-\frac{C_{1}|\xi+\sigma_{-}t|^{2}}{2(1+t)}}e^{-Ct}d\xi\leq C\left(\varepsilon_{r}\delta_{R}+\delta_{R}\right)\delta_{C}e^{-Ct}.\end{aligned}$ On the other hand, if $\xi+\sigma_{-}t\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)=-\frac{1}{2}|\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})|(1+t)$ , then we have (4.7) $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} e^{-C\varepsilon_{r}|\xi+\sigma_{-}t-\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)|}=&e^{-C\varepsilon_{r}|\xi+\sigma_{-}t-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)|}e^{-\frac{1}{2}C\varepsilon_{r}|\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})|(1+t)}.\end{aligned}$ Therefore, by ( 4.7 ) and Lemma 2.4 , we obtain (4.8) $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\int_{\xi\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)-\sigma_{-}t}\left(|v_{\xi}^{R}||v^{C}-v_{m}|+|v_{\xi}^{C}||v^{R}-v_{m}|\right)\,d\xi\\
&\,\,\leq C\delta_{C}\delta_{R}(\varepsilon_{r}+1)\int_{\xi\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)-\sigma_{-}t}e^{-C\varepsilon_{r}|\xi+\sigma_{-}t-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)|}e^{-C\varepsilon_{r}|\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})|(1+t)}d\xi\\
&\,\,\leq C\delta_{C}\frac{\delta_{R}+\varepsilon_{r}\delta_{R}}{\varepsilon_{r}}e^{-C\varepsilon_{r}t}.\end{aligned}$ Combining ( 4.6 ) and ( 4.8 ), and using $\varepsilon_{r}\leq 1$ , we have $\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{4}\,d\xi\leq C\delta_{R}\delta_{C}e^{-Ct}+C\delta_{C}\frac{\delta_{R}}{\varepsilon_{r}}e^{-C\varepsilon_{r}t}.$ For $J_{5}$ , we first split it as: $\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{5}\,d\xi=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|v_{\xi}^{R}||v^{S}-v^{*}|+|v_{\xi}^{S}||v^{R}-v_{m}|\,d\xi$ $\displaystyle=\left(\int_{0\leq\xi\leq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)-\sigma_{-}t}+\int_{\xi\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)-\sigma_{-}t}\right)\left(|v_{\xi}^{R}||v^{S}-v^{*}|+|v_{\xi}^{S}||v^{R}-v_{m}|\right)d\xi.$ Since $\xi+\sigma_{-}t\leq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)<0$ , we obtain (4.9) $\displaystyle\xi-(\sigma-\sigma_{-})t-X(t)-\beta\leq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)-\frac{3}{4}\sigma t-\beta\leq-\frac{3}{4}\sigma t<0.$ Using an argument similar to that for ( 4.5 ), together with ( 4.9 ), Lemma 2.3 , and ( 2.11 ), we have (4.10) $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\int_{0\leq\xi\leq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)-\sigma_{-}t}\left(|v_{\xi}^{R}||v^{S}-v^{*}|+|v_{\xi}^{S}||v^{R}-v_{m}|\right)\,d\xi\\
&\quad\leq C\delta_{R}\delta_{S}(\varepsilon_{r}+\delta_{S})\int_{0\leq\xi\leq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)-\sigma_{-}t}e^{-\frac{1}{2}C\delta_{S}|\xi-(\sigma-\sigma_{-})t-X(t)-\beta|}e^{-C\delta_{S}t}d\xi\\
&\quad\leq C\delta_{R}\delta_{S}(\varepsilon_{r}+\delta_{S})e^{-C\delta_{S}t}.\end{aligned}$ On the other hand, for $\xi+\sigma_{-}t\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)$ , using Lemma 2.3 , ( 4.7 ) and Lemma 2.5 , we obtain (4.11) $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\int_{\xi\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)-\sigma_{-}t}\left(|v_{\xi}^{R}||v^{S}-v^{*}|+|v_{\xi}^{S}||v^{R}-v_{m}|\right)\,d\xi\\
&\quad\leq C\delta_{S}\delta_{R}(\varepsilon_{r}+\delta_{S})e^{-C\varepsilon_{r}t}\int_{\xi\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)-\sigma_{-}t}e^{-C\varepsilon_{r}|\xi+\sigma_{-}t-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)|}\,d\xi\\
&\quad\leq C\delta_{S}\delta_{R}\frac{(\varepsilon_{r}+\delta_{S})}{\varepsilon_{r}}e^{-C\varepsilon_{r}t}.\end{aligned}$ Combining ( 4.10 ) and ( 4.11 ), we obtain the desired estimate. Now, we consider $J_{6}$ . Let $\alpha:=\frac{1}{2}(-\sigma_{-}+(\sigma-\sigma_{-}))=\frac{\sigma-2\sigma_{-}}{2}$ . By Lemma 2.4 and Lemma 2.5 , we obtain $\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{6}$ $\displaystyle=\left(\int_{0}^{\alpha t}+\int_{\alpha t}^{\infty}\right)|v_{\xi}^{C}||v^{S}-v^{*}|+|v_{\xi}^{S}||v^{C}-v^{*}|\,d\xi$ $\displaystyle\leq C\delta_{C}\delta_{S}\int_{0}^{\alpha t}e^{-C\delta_{S}|\xi-(\sigma-\sigma_{-})t-X(t)-\beta|}\,d\xi+C\delta_{C}\delta_{S}\int_{\alpha t}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{C_{1}\left|\xi+\sigma_{-}t\right|^{2}}{1+t}}\,d\xi$ $\displaystyle\leq C\delta_{C}\delta_{S}e^{-C\delta_{S}t}+C\delta_{C}\delta_{S}e^{-Ct}.$ $\bullet$ Proof of ( 4.3 ) From ( 4.2 ), we deduce that $\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{6}\|J_{i}\|_{L^{1}}^{4/3}\,d\tau$ $\displaystyle\leq C\big((\varepsilon_{r}\delta_{R})^{1/6}+\delta_{BL}^{1/3}\delta_{R}^{4/3}\big)+C\big(\delta_{C}^{4/3}+\delta_{S}^{4/3}\big)\big(\delta_{BL}^{4/3}+\delta_{BL}^{1/3}\big)$ $\displaystyle+C\left((\delta_{R}\delta_{C})^{4/3}+\delta_{C}^{4/3}\left(\frac{\delta_{R}}{\varepsilon_{r}}\right)^{4/3}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{r}}\right)$ $\displaystyle+C\left(\delta_{R}^{4/3}\delta_{S}^{1/3}(\varepsilon_{r}+\delta_{S})^{4/3}+C\delta_{S}^{4/3}(\varepsilon_{r}+\delta_{S})^{4/3}\left(\frac{\delta_{R}}{\varepsilon_{r}}\right)^{4/3}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{r}}\right)+C(\delta_{R}^{4/3}\delta_{S}^{1/3}(\varepsilon_{r}+\delta_{S})^{4/3}).$ Using the smallness of $\delta_{0}$ and $\varepsilon_{r}$ , we obtain $\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{6}\|J_{i}\|_{L^{1}}^{4/3}\,d\tau\leq C\delta_{0}+C(\varepsilon_{r}\delta_{R})^{1/6}+C\delta_{0}\left(\frac{\delta_{R}}{\varepsilon_{r}}\right)^{4/3}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{r}}.$ ∎ Lemma 4.2 . Under the assumptions of Proposition 3.2 , there exist positive constants $c$ and $C$ such that the following estimates hold: (4.12) $\displaystyle\begin{split}&\begin{aligned} &\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|(u_{\xi}^{C},u_{\xi\xi}^{C})||\bar{v}-v^{C}|\,d\xi\\
&\qquad\leq C\delta_{C}\left[\delta_{BL}\left(e^{-ct}+\frac{\ln(1+\delta_{BL}t)}{(1+t)}\right)+(\delta_{R}+\delta_{S})e^{-ct}+\delta_{S}^{3/4}e^{-c\delta_{S}t}+\frac{\delta_{R}}{\varepsilon_{r}}e^{-c\varepsilon_{r}t}\right],\\
\end{aligned}\end{split}$ (4.13) $\displaystyle\begin{split}&\begin{aligned} &\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|v_{\xi}^{BL}||v_{\xi}^{R}|\,d\xi\leq C\delta_{BL}\frac{(\delta_{R}\varepsilon_{r})^{1/8}}{(1+t)^{7/8}},\\
&\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|v_{\xi}^{BL}|\big(|v_{\xi}^{C}|+|u_{\xi}^{C}|\big)\,d\xi\leq C\delta_{BL}\delta_{C}\left(e^{-ct}+\frac{1}{1+\delta_{BL}t}\right),\end{aligned}\\
&\begin{aligned} &\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|v_{\xi}^{BL}||v_{\xi}^{S}|\,d\xi\leq C\delta_{BL}\delta_{S}\left(\delta_{BL}e^{-c\delta_{S}t}+\frac{\delta_{S}}{1+\delta_{BL}t}\right),\\
&\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|v_{\xi}^{R}|\big(|v_{\xi}^{C}|+|u_{\xi}^{C}|\big)\,d\xi\leq C\delta_{C}\left(\varepsilon_{r}\delta_{R}e^{-ct}+\frac{\delta_{R}\varepsilon_{r}}{\varepsilon_{r}}e^{-c\varepsilon_{r}t}\right),\\
&\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|v_{\xi}^{R}||v_{\xi}^{S}|\,d\xi\leq C\varepsilon_{r}\delta_{R}\delta_{S}e^{-c\delta_{S}t}+C\delta_{S}^{2}\frac{\delta_{R}\varepsilon_{r}}{\varepsilon_{r}}e^{-c\varepsilon_{r}t},\\
&\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|v_{\xi}^{S}|\big(|v_{\xi}^{C}|+|u_{\xi}^{C}|\big)\,d\xi\leq C\delta_{S}\delta_{C}e^{-ct}.\end{aligned}\end{split}$ Consequently, we have (4.14) $\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\||(u_{\xi}^{C},u_{\xi\xi}^{C})||\bar{v}-v^{C}|\|_{L^{1}}^{4/3}+\sum_{i\neq j}\||(v_{i})_{\xi}|(|(v_{j})_{\xi}|+|(u_{j})_{\xi}|)\|_{L^{1}}^{4/3}\,d\tau$ $\displaystyle\leq C\delta_{0}\big(1+(\delta_{R}\varepsilon_{r})^{1/6}\big)+C\delta_{0}\left(\frac{\delta_{R}}{\varepsilon_{r}}\right)^{4/3}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{r}},$ where $U_{i}$ , $i=1,2,3,4$ are defined in ( 3.6 ). Since the proof of Lemma 4.2 is similar to that of Lemma 4.1 , we defer it to the Appendix B . 4.2. $L^{2}$ Interaction Estimates Now, we give the $L^{2}$ estimates of $J_{i}$ , where $J_{i}$ are defined in ( 4.1 ). We again emphasize that the estimates established in ( 4.15 ) of Lemma 4.3 , as well as those in ( 4.23 ) of Lemma 4.4 , remain valid even when the strength of the boundary layer solution is large. Lemma 4.3 . Under the hypotheses as in Proposition 3.2 , there exist positive constants $c$ and $C$ such that the following estimates hold: (4.15) $\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{1}^{2}d\xi\leq C\frac{\delta_{R}^{\frac{3}{8}}\varepsilon_{r}^{\frac{1}{8}}\delta_{BL}^{\frac{5}{4}}}{(1+t)^{\frac{13}{8}}}+C\frac{(\varepsilon_{r}\delta_{R})^{\frac{1}{4}}\delta_{BL}}{(1+t)^{\frac{7}{4}}},$ $\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{2}^{2}d\xi\leq\delta_{BL}^{2}\delta_{C}^{2}e^{-ct}+C\delta_{C}^{2}\delta_{BL}^{2}\frac{1}{(1+\delta_{BL}t)^{2}},$ $\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{3}^{2}d\xi\leq C(\delta_{BL}^{4}\delta_{S}+\delta_{BL}^{2}\delta_{S}^{3})e^{-c\delta_{S}t}+C\delta_{S}^{3}\delta_{BL}^{2}\frac{1}{(1+\delta_{BL}t)^{2}},$ $\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{4}^{2}d\xi\leq C\left(\varepsilon_{r}^{2}\delta_{R}^{2}\delta_{C}^{2}+\delta_{R}^{2}\delta_{C}^{2}\right)e^{-ct}+C\delta_{C}^{2}\frac{\delta_{R}^{2}}{\varepsilon_{r}}e^{-c\varepsilon_{r}t},$ $\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{5}^{2}d\xi\leq\left(\varepsilon_{r}^{2}\delta_{R}^{2}\delta_{S}+\delta_{R}^{2}\delta_{S}^{3}\right)e^{-c\delta_{S}t}+C\delta_{S}^{2}\frac{\delta_{R}^{2}}{\varepsilon_{r}}e^{-c\varepsilon_{r}t},$ $\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{6}^{2}d\xi\leq C\delta_{C}^{2}\delta_{S}e^{-c\delta_{S}t}+\delta_{C}^{2}\delta_{S}^{2}e^{-ct},$ where $J_{i}(i=1,2,\cdots,6)$ are defined as in ( 4.1 ). Consequently, we have (4.16) $\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{6}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{i}^{2}\,d\xi\,d\tau\leq C\delta_{0}+C(\delta_{R}\varepsilon_{r})^{2}+C\delta_{0}\left(\frac{\delta_{R}}{\varepsilon_{r}}\right)^{2}.$ Proof. $\bullet$ (Estimate of $J_{1}$ ) First, we split $J_{1}$ as (4.17) $\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{1}^{2}d\xi\leq 2\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|v_{\xi}^{BL}|^{2}|v^{R}-v_{*}|^{2}d\xi+2\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|v_{\xi}^{R}|^{2}|v^{BL}-v_{*}|^{2}d\xi.$ For the first term on the right-hand side of ( 4.17 ), we use Hölder’s inequality to have for any $t>0$ and $y>0$ , $v^{R}(t,y)-v_{*}=\int_{0}^{y}v_{\xi}^{R}\,d\xi\leq\|v_{\xi}^{R}\|_{L^{8}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}y^{7/8}.$ By Lemma 2.3 , we have $\displaystyle\|v_{\xi}^{R}\|_{L^{8}}$ $\displaystyle\leq\|v_{\xi}^{R}\|_{L^{8}}^{\frac{1}{14}}\|v_{\xi}^{R}\|_{L^{8}}^{\frac{13}{14}}\leq C(\delta_{R}\varepsilon_{r}^{\frac{7}{8}})^{\frac{1}{14}}\left(\delta_{R}^{\frac{1}{8}}(1+t)^{-\frac{7}{8}})\right)^{\frac{13}{14}}\leq C\delta_{R}^{\frac{3}{16}}\varepsilon_{r}^{\frac{1}{16}}\left(1+t\right)^{-\frac{13}{16}}.$ Thus, we obtain $\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|v_{\xi}^{BL}|^{2}|v^{R}-v_{*}|^{2}\,d\xi$ $\displaystyle\leq\|v_{\xi}^{R}\|_{L^{8}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\xi^{7/4}|v_{\xi}^{BL}|^{2}\,d\xi$ $\displaystyle\leq\frac{C\delta_{R}^{\frac{3}{8}}\varepsilon_{r}^{\frac{1}{8}}}{(1+t)^{\frac{13}{8}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\xi^{7/4}\frac{\delta_{BL}^{4}}{(1+\delta_{BL}\xi)^{4}}\,d\xi\leq\frac{C\delta_{R}^{\frac{3}{8}}\varepsilon_{r}^{\frac{1}{8}}\delta_{BL}^{\frac{5}{4}}}{(1+t)^{\frac{13}{8}}}.$ For the second term of ( 4.17 ), it follows from ( 4.4 ) and Lemma 2.1 that $\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|v_{\xi}^{R}|^{2}|v^{BL}-v_{*}|^{2}d\xi$ $\displaystyle\leq C\frac{(\varepsilon_{r}\delta_{R})^{\frac{1}{4}}}{(1+t)^{\frac{7}{4}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\frac{\delta_{BL}^{2}}{\left(1+\delta_{BL}\xi\right)^{2}}d\xi\leq C\frac{(\varepsilon_{r}\delta_{R})^{\frac{1}{4}}\delta_{BL}}{(1+t)^{\frac{7}{4}}}.$ Therefore, we have $\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{1}^{2}d\xi\leq C\frac{\delta_{R}^{\frac{3}{8}}\varepsilon_{r}^{\frac{1}{8}}\delta_{BL}^{\frac{5}{4}}}{(1+t)^{\frac{13}{8}}}+C\frac{(\varepsilon_{r}\delta_{R})^{\frac{1}{4}}\delta_{BL}}{(1+t)^{\frac{7}{4}}}.$ $\bullet$ (Estimate of $J_{2}$ ) We split $J_{2}$ as (4.18) $\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{2}^{2}d\xi\leq 2\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|v_{\xi}^{BL}|^{2}|v^{C}-v_{m}|^{2}d\xi+2\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|v_{\xi}^{C}|^{2}|v^{BL}-v_{*}|^{2}d\xi.$ For the first term on the right-hand side of ( 4.18 ), we use Lemma 2.1 and ( 4.5 ) to have $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|v_{\xi}^{BL}|^{2}|v^{C}-v_{m}|^{2}d\xi=\left(\int_{0}^{-\frac{\sigma_{-}t}{2}}+\int_{-\frac{\sigma_{-}t}{2}}^{\infty}\right)|v_{\xi}^{BL}|^{2}|v^{C}-v_{m}|^{2}d\xi\\
&\quad\leq C\delta_{BL}^{4}\delta_{C}^{2}\int_{0}^{-\frac{\sigma_{-}t}{2}}e^{-\frac{C_{1}|\xi+\sigma_{-}t|^{2}}{1+t}}e^{-Ct}d\xi+C\delta_{BL}^{4}\delta_{C}^{2}\int_{-\frac{\sigma_{-}t}{2}}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(1+\delta_{BL}\xi)^{4}}d\xi\\
&\quad\leq C\delta_{BL}^{4}\delta_{C}^{2}e^{-ct}+C\delta_{BL}^{3}\delta_{C}^{2}\frac{1}{(1+\delta_{BL}t)^{3}}.\end{aligned}$ Similarly, by Lemma 2.1 and ( 4.5 ), we estimate the second term in ( 4.18 ) as $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|v_{\xi}^{C}|^{2}|v^{BL}-v_{*}|^{2}d\xi=\left(\int_{0}^{-\frac{\sigma_{-}t}{2}}+\int_{-\frac{\sigma_{-}t}{2}}^{\infty}\right)|v_{\xi}^{C}|^{2}|v^{BL}-v_{*}|^{2}d\xi\\
&\quad\leq C\delta_{BL}^{2}\delta_{C}^{2}\int_{0}^{-\frac{\sigma_{-}t}{2}}e^{-\frac{C_{1}|\xi+\sigma_{-}t|^{2}}{1+t}}e^{-ct}d\xi+C\delta_{C}^{2}\frac{1}{1+t}\int_{-\frac{\sigma_{-}t}{2}}^{\infty}\frac{\delta_{BL}^{2}}{(1+\delta_{BL}\xi)^{2}}e^{-\frac{2C_{1}|\xi+\sigma_{-}t|^{2}}{1+t}}d\xi\\
&\quad\leq C\delta_{BL}^{2}\delta_{C}^{2}e^{-Ct}+C\delta_{C}^{2}\delta^{2}_{BL}\frac{1}{(1+\delta_{BL}t)^{2}}\frac{1}{1+t}\int_{-\frac{\sigma_{-}t}{2}}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{2C_{1}|\xi+\sigma_{-}t|^{2}}{1+t}}d\xi\\
&\quad\leq C\delta_{BL}^{2}\delta_{C}^{2}e^{-Ct}+C\delta_{C}^{2}\delta^{2}_{BL}\frac{1}{(1+\delta_{BL}t)^{2}}.\end{aligned}$ Combining the above estimates, we have $\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{2}^{2}d\xi\leq C\delta_{BL}^{2}\delta_{C}^{2}e^{-Ct}+C\delta_{C}^{2}\delta_{BL}^{2}\frac{1}{(1+\delta_{BL}t)^{2}}.$ $\bullet$ (Estimate of $J_{3}$ ) We split $J_{3}$ as (4.19) $\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{3}^{2}d\xi\leq 2\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|v_{\xi}^{BL}|^{2}|v^{S}-v^{*}|^{2}d\xi+2\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|v_{\xi}^{S}|^{2}|v^{BL}-v_{*}|^{2}d\xi.$ For the first term of ( 4.19 ), using Lemma 2.1 , Lemma 2.5 , and ( 4.9 ), we have $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|v_{\xi}^{BL}|^{2}|v^{S}-v^{*}|^{2}d\xi&\leq\left(\int_{0}^{bt}+\int_{bt}^{\infty}\right)|v_{\xi}^{BL}|^{2}|v^{S}-v^{*}|^{2}d\xi\\
&\leq C\delta_{BL}^{4}\delta_{S}e^{-C\delta_{S}t}+C\delta_{BL}^{3}\delta_{S}^{2}\frac{1}{(1+\delta_{BL}t)^{3}}.\end{aligned}$ Similarly, the second term of ( 4.19 ) can be estimated as follows: $\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|v_{\xi}^{S}|^{2}|v^{BL}-v_{*}|^{2}\,d\xi$ $\displaystyle=\left(\int_{0}^{bt}+\int_{bt}^{\infty}\right)|v_{\xi}^{S}|^{2}|v^{BL}-v_{*}|^{2}\,d\xi$ $\displaystyle\leq C\delta_{BL}^{2}\delta_{S}^{3}e^{-C\delta_{S}t}+\int_{bt}^{\infty}|v_{\xi}^{S}|^{2}|v^{BL}-v_{*}|^{2}\,d\xi,$ where $b=\frac{(\sigma-\sigma_{-})}{2}$ . Now, we use Fubini’s theorem to have $\displaystyle\int_{bt}^{\infty}|(v^{S})_{\xi}|^{2}|v^{BL}-v_{*}|^{2}d\xi=$ $\displaystyle-\int_{bt}^{\infty}|(v^{S})_{\xi}^{-X}|^{2}\int_{\xi}^{\infty}\left(|v^{BL}-v_{*}|^{2}\right)_{y}(y)dyd\xi$ $\displaystyle=$ $\displaystyle-2\int_{bt}^{\infty}v^{BL}_{y}(v^{BL}-v_{*})(y)\int_{bt}^{y}|(v^{S})_{\xi}|^{2}\,d\xi\,dy$ $\displaystyle\leq$ $\displaystyle 2\left(\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|(v^{S})_{\xi}|^{2}d\xi\right)\left(\int_{bt}^{\infty}|v^{BL}_{y}||v^{BL}-v_{*}|dy\right)$ $\displaystyle\leq$ $\displaystyle C\delta^{3}_{S}\int_{bt}^{\infty}\frac{\delta_{BL}^{3}}{(1+\delta_{BL}y)^{3}}dy$ $\displaystyle\leq$ $\displaystyle C\delta_{S}^{3}\delta_{BL}^{2}\frac{1}{(1+\delta_{BL}t)^{2}}.$ Therefore, we obtain (4.20) $\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|v_{\xi}^{S}|^{2}|v^{BL}-v_{*}|^{2}\,d\xi\leq C\delta_{BL}^{2}\delta_{S}^{3}e^{-C\delta_{S}t}+C\delta_{S}^{3}\delta_{BL}^{2}\frac{1}{(1+\delta_{BL}t)^{2}}.$ Thus, we have $\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{3}^{2}d\xi\leq C(\delta_{BL}^{4}\delta_{S}+\delta_{BL}^{2}\delta_{S}^{3})e^{-C\delta_{S}t}+C\delta_{S}^{3}\delta_{BL}^{2}\frac{1}{(1+\delta_{BL}t)^{2}}.$ $\bullet$ (Estimate of $J_{4}$ ) We split $J_{4}$ as $\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{4}^{2}d\xi$ $\displaystyle\,\,\leq 2\int_{0\leq\xi\leq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)-\sigma_{-}t}\big(|v_{\xi}^{R}|^{2}|v^{C}-v_{m}|^{2}+|v_{\xi}^{C}|^{2}|v^{R}-v_{m}|^{2}\big)\,d\xi$ $\displaystyle\,\,\quad+2\int_{\xi\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)-\sigma_{-}t}\big(|v_{\xi}^{R}|^{2}|v^{C}-v_{m}|^{2}+|v_{\xi}^{C}|^{2}|v^{R}-v_{m}|^{2}\big)d\xi.$ By Lemma 2.3 and ( 4.5 ), we obtain $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\int_{0\leq\xi\leq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)-\sigma_{-}t}\left(|v_{\xi}^{R}|^{2}|v^{C}-v_{m}|^{2}+|v_{\xi}^{C}|^{2}|v^{R}-v_{m}|^{2}\right)d\xi\\
&\quad\leq C\left((\varepsilon_{r}\delta_{R})^{2}\delta_{C}^{2}+\delta_{R}^{2}\delta_{C}^{2}\right)e^{-Ct}.\end{aligned}$ On the other hand, it follows from Lemma 2.3 , Lemma 2.4 and ( 4.7 ) that $\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\int_{\xi\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)-\sigma_{-}t}\left(|v_{\xi}^{R}|^{2}|v^{C}-v_{m}|^{2}+|v_{\xi}^{C}|^{2}|v^{R}-v_{m}|^{2}\right)d\xi\\
&\,\,\leq C\delta_{C}^{2}\delta_{R}^{2}(1+\varepsilon_{r}^{2})\int_{\xi\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)-\sigma_{-}t}e^{-C\varepsilon_{r}|\xi+\sigma_{-}t-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)|}e^{-C\varepsilon_{r}|\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})|(1+t)}d\xi\\
&\,\,\leq C\delta_{C}^{2}\frac{\delta_{R}^{2}(1+\varepsilon_{r}^{2})}{\varepsilon_{r}}e^{-C\varepsilon_{r}t}.\end{aligned}$ Therefore, together with the fact that $\varepsilon_{r}\leq 1$ , we have $\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{4}^{2}d\xi\leq C\left(\varepsilon_{r}^{2}\delta_{R}^{2}\delta_{C}^{2}+\delta_{R}^{2}\delta_{C}^{2}\right)e^{-Ct}+C\delta_{C}^{2}\frac{\delta_{R}^{2}}{\varepsilon_{r}}e^{-C\varepsilon_{r}t}.$ $\bullet$ (Estimate of $J_{5}$ ) First, we decompose $J_{5}$ as follows: $\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{5}^{2}d\xi\leq 2\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\left(|v_{\xi}^{R}|^{2}|v^{S}-v^{*}|^{2}+|v_{\xi}^{S}|^{2}|v^{R}-v_{m}|^{2}\right)d\xi$ $\displaystyle\leq C\left(\int_{0\leq\xi\leq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)-\sigma_{-}t}+\int_{\xi\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)-\sigma_{-}t}\right)|v_{\xi}^{R}|^{2}|v^{S}-v^{*}|^{2}d\xi$ $\displaystyle\quad+C\left(\int_{0\leq\xi\leq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)-\sigma_{-}t}+\int_{\xi\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)-\sigma_{-}t}\right)|v_{\xi}^{S}|^{2}|v^{R}-v_{m}|^{2}d\xi.$ Since $\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)<\beta+\sigma t$ , by Lemma 2.5 and ( 4.9 ), we deduce that $\displaystyle\int_{0\leq\xi\leq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)-\sigma_{-}t}|v_{\xi}^{R}|^{2}|v^{S}-v^{*}|^{2}d\xi+\int_{\xi\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)-\sigma_{-}t}|v_{\xi}^{R}|^{2}|v^{S}-v^{*}|^{2}d\xi$ $\displaystyle\leq C(\varepsilon_{r}\delta_{R})^{2}\delta_{S}e^{-C\delta_{S}t}+C\delta_{S}^{2}\int_{\xi\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)-\sigma_{-}t}|v_{\xi}^{R}|^{2}\,d\xi$ $\displaystyle\leq C(\varepsilon_{r}\delta_{R})^{2}\delta_{S}e^{-C\delta_{S}t}+C\delta_{R}^{2}\varepsilon_{r}e^{-C\varepsilon_{r}t},$ and similarly, (4.21) $\displaystyle\int_{0\leq\xi\leq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)-\sigma_{-}t}|v_{\xi}^{S}|^{2}|v^{R}-v_{m}|^{2}d\xi+\int_{\xi\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)-\sigma_{-}t}|v_{\xi}^{S}|^{2}|v^{R}-v_{m}|^{2}d\xi$ $\displaystyle\leq C\delta_{R}^{2}\delta_{S}^{3}e^{-C\delta_{S}t}+C\delta_{S}^{4}\int_{\xi\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(u_{m},\theta_{m})(1+t)-\sigma_{-}t}|v^{R}-v_{m}|^{2}\,d\xi$ $\displaystyle\leq C\delta_{R}^{2}\delta_{S}^{3}e^{-C\delta_{S}t}+C\delta_{S}^{4}\frac{\delta_{R}^{2}}{\varepsilon_{r}}e^{-C\varepsilon_{r}t}.$ Combining the above estimates, we have $\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}J_{5}^{2}d\xi\leq C\left(\varepsilon_{r}^{2}\delta_{R}^{2}\delta_{S}+\delta_{R}^{2}\delta_{S}^{3}\right)e^{-C\delta_{S}t}+C\delta_{S}^{2}\frac{\delta_{R}^{2}}{\varepsilon_{r}}e^{-C\varepsilon_{r}t}.$ $\bullet$ (Estimate of $J_{6}$ ) Let $\alpha=\frac{1}{2}(-\sigma_{-}+(\sigma-\sigma_{-}))=\frac{\sigma-2\sigma_{-}}{2}$ .
